The intellectual lineage extends beyond GraphRAG to program comprehension research (Storey 2005; Müller and Klashinsky’s earlier work on software architecture recovery) which has long argued that source code alone is insufficient for understanding large legacy systems — derived representations are necessary.

-----

## Pattern 4: Domain Ontology as Independent Substrate

### Context

A modernization with structural graph and [semantic index](#gl-semantic-index) (Pattern 3) in place. The legacy is treated as behavioural oracle (Pattern 2). Agents reason over real artifacts of the legacy system. What those substrates carry is the legacy’s *structure* and its *behaviour*. What they cannot carry is its *language*.

Domain-Driven Design is, at its centre, a discipline about language. The **[ubiquitous language](#gl-ubiquitous-language)** is the shared vocabulary used by the team and encoded in the system within a single [bounded context](#gl-bounded-context) — the same words appearing in conversation, in documentation, in commit messages, in the names of types and methods and variables. Eric Evans’ argument (2003) is that code which encodes the domain’s vocabulary literally — `CustomerAccount.applyPayment(transaction)`, not `CUSTMAST.PROC1(REC)` — is the mechanism by which shared understanding survives the gap between domain experts and developers. The code becomes a model the team — including the agents that read and write it — can reason about together. When the model evolves, the language evolves; when the language evolves, the code follows. The discipline holds the three in alignment.

Where bounded contexts have to integrate, DDD names a distinct artifact: the **[published language](#gl-published-language)** (Evans, 2003, p. 330) — a well-documented shared vocabulary that serves as the medium of communication between contexts, with each context translating to and from it. Inter-organizational standards like iCalendar, vCard, BIAN, FIBO, and ACORD are canonical examples. The two artifacts are not the same: ubiquitous language is the team’s working vocabulary within a context; published language is the agreed protocol between contexts.

The catalog so far has recovered what the legacy carries: behaviour from Pattern 2, structure from Pattern 3. The third recovery — language, in both forms — is the work of this pattern.

### Problem

A system that has been running for years or decades carries **ontology drift**: the accumulated divergence between the concepts the legacy code names and the concepts that would coherently describe the domain today. Drift takes several forms simultaneously. The same business concept is named differently across modules — *customer*, *client*, *party*, *stakeholder* — sometimes referring to the same entity, sometimes to subtly different ones. The same word means different things in different places — *account* as a customer relationship in one module, an accounting period in another, a numeric key in a third. Old concepts persist long after the business has retired them; new concepts have no representation at all and are handled by overloading existing fields with conventions no schema enforces. The legacy faithfully implements all of these contradictions, often in tension with each other, almost always without anyone noticing.

*Ontology drift* is distinct from *semantic drift* (Patterns 15 and 17), which describes runtime divergence between modernized and legacy systems during verification. Ontology drift is the *origin condition* — the drift that has already occurred inside the legacy itself, before the modernization begins.

The trap is that modernization through structural and behavioural fidelity alone *preserves* the drift. Agents can faithfully translate a paragraph that computes “balance” without ever asking which of the three definitions of “balance” the paragraph implements. The translation can be flawless and the result still be unmodernized in DDD’s sense: the new C# type is called `Balance`, the new variable is called `accountBalance`, the new method is called `recalculateBalance`, and none of them mean what the business means by *balance* today. The architecture is current and the team is no closer to a shared language than they were before. Behavioural equivalence has been achieved without recovering what the behaviour is *about*.

Anthony Alcaraz has argued the broader version of this for agentic systems. By *orchestration* he means the engineering of how agents compose — multi-agent workflows, supervision hierarchies, tool-routing, the state machines that govern when one agent defers to another. His claim is that ontological grounding is prior: a system whose composed agents reason over incoherent concepts cannot be recovered by any orchestration pattern, however well-designed. The argument lands with particular force in legacy modernization, where the legacy has had decades to drift.

### Forces

Behavioural recovery is necessary (Patterns 1, 2, 3) and tractable: the legacy is observable, queryable, runnable. Ontological recovery is harder, and it requires sources the legacy alone does not provide. At least four kinds of input feed it: **domain experts** (whose tacit knowledge is hard to elicit and whose time is scarce), **business artifacts** (product documentation, regulatory definitions, internal policy — often partial and contradictory), **industry taxonomies** (NAICS, BIAN, FIBO, ACORD, GS1 — sector vocabularies the business operates within, available as the published languages for inter-context and inter-organizational communication), and **operational knowledge** (runtime telemetry, production behaviour, the working understanding operators have built — often retaining truths the source code has lost). These sources are partial, sometimes contradictory, require human judgement to reconcile, and cannot be inferred from the legacy alone.

A second asymmetry sits underneath the first: *naming* in code has disproportionate leverage. A variable named correctly is a small artifact carrying a large amount of shared understanding; a variable named wrongly is a small artifact carrying confusion forward into every future change. Behaviour can be tested. Naming can only be agreed. The modernization that gets naming wrong builds clean architecture on a misaligned vocabulary, and the misalignment compounds with every new feature.

### Pattern

Treat the recovered language of the domain as an independent substrate of the modernization. The substrate carries the ubiquitous language within each bounded context — the vocabulary the team commits to use in code, conversation, and design — and the published languages between contexts, including industry taxonomies the organization adopts or adapts as inter-context protocols.

The substrate is **organized by subdomain**. A subdomain is a region of the problem space — a coherent part of the business such as claims, billing, or pricing — and the ontology groups concepts by the subdomain they belong to, because a subdomain is precisely the scope within which a set of concepts means one consistent thing. This organization is what makes the ontology diagnostic: when a single concept (say, *Policy*) carries two incompatible definitions in the substrate, the split is evidence of two subdomains — or of one subdomain wrongly fused in the legacy. A bounded context is the solution-space home for, ideally, one subdomain, within which one ubiquitous language applies; in legacy systems that alignment is rarely clean, and the gap between the subdomains the ontology reveals and the contexts the legacy actually implemented is itself a primary finding (Pattern 6 turns that finding into a constraint on context relationships).

**Published languages already exist in the legacy, undeclared.** A mainframe estate that has been running for decades has *de facto* published languages everywhere: the DCLGEN copybook shared between two programs, the COMMAREA layout that CICS transactions pass between contexts, the file format on a batch-step boundary, the queue message schema another team's job depends on. None of these are *called* published languages, but they fit Evans' definition exactly — documented (sometimes), shared (always), used as the medium between two contexts. They are structurally fragile, often technically expressed rather than semantically expressed, and frequently lack a central registry. Modernization is rarely the work of inventing published languages from scratch; more often it is the work of recovering the implicit ones, naming them, deciding which to formalise, which to evolve, and which to retire. Where a cross-context side-effect surface exists in the legacy (Pattern 5), an implicit published language is almost certainly carrying it. Recovery is the first move; formalisation is the second.

**Published-language strategy depends on subdomain type.** The strategic-design decision an organization makes for each context is not free-form. For **generic** subdomains (payroll, address validation, currency conversion — capabilities the business does not differentiate on), the right strategy is almost always to *adopt* an industry-standard published language. These external vocabularies — NAICS, FIBO, ACORD, GS1, ISO 20022, and their kin — are published languages the organization does not author and cannot negotiate: they are maintained by standards bodies, and the system either conforms to them or pays a translation cost at every integration. Adopting saves coordination cost forever — every integration partner already speaks the standard, every package vendor maps to it. For **core** subdomains (the business's actual differentiator), the right strategy is to *invent* the published language — the vocabulary embodies the competitive understanding of the domain, and adopting an industry vocabulary would commoditise the edge. For **supporting** subdomains (necessary but not differentiating), the right strategy is to *adapt* — start from an industry standard, extend it where business-specific concepts apply, retire pieces that do not. The choice is itself a strategic-design decision (Pattern 1) and a downstream determinant of which Pattern 9 scaffold the bounded context lands in.

*Independent* here is a separation of concern, not of infrastructure. The substrate has its own schema, its own change cadence, its own ownership, its own lifecycle, distinct from the structural graph (Pattern 3) and from the legacy implementation artifacts it draws on. In practice it is most naturally implemented as a dedicated property graph (its own schema, possibly its own database instance) within the same GraphRAG infrastructure Pattern 3 establishes. Sharing the technology is an engineering convenience; sharing the schema would be a category error.

**Recovery.** Recovery from the legacy is partial, and it divides cleanly along the deterministic / probabilistic / human line this catalog draws everywhere. Vocabulary inference from comments, display literals, naming conventions, the intermediate representation, and the data layer’s DDL — DB2 schemas, VSAM definitions, DCLGEN copybooks — provides candidate terms. The data layer often preserves domain vocabulary better than the procedural code does: DDL columns frequently retain canonical business terms that working-storage variables in COBOL paragraphs have abbreviated, prefixed, or renamed for technical convenience. This candidate-gathering is the probabilistic, machine-scale half of the work: an agent reading the graph and semantic index (Pattern 3) across the whole estate proposes candidate terms, clusters the *customer / client / party* variants that may name one concept, and flags the words that change meaning between modules — surfacing drift no human could find by reading a million lines. What the agent cannot do is decide. The architect or domain expert validates each candidate, refines vocabulary, reconciles the drift the agent surfaced, and articulates the canonical concepts the modernized system will encode — because strategic value and conceptual coherence are business judgments, not inferences from the source. Workshop techniques like [Event Storming](#gl-event-storming) (Brandolini) accelerate the human side — domain experts, developers, and operators in a room mapping events, commands, and [aggregates](#gl-aggregate) against shared vocabulary. Agents surface candidates at a scale humans cannot; humans reconcile them with a judgment agents cannot. These are starting points, not conclusions.

**What the substrate enables.** Once established, the ontology is the artifact a number of activities operate against. *Knowledge crunching* — Evans’ term for the iterative work of moving from naive descriptions of the domain to deeper, sharper models — has a destination: refinements land here, not in someone’s notebook. *Disambiguation* — when two stakeholders disagree about what *active customer* means, the ontology either resolves it by surfacing the canonical definition or makes the disagreement legible enough that the ontology itself needs revision. *Boundary evidence* — points where a word changes meaning across the legacy are evidence of bounded-context boundaries the modernization must honour, one of several inputs to decomposition (alongside the capability map of Pattern 1, the structural clusters of Pattern 3, and the slice signals of Pattern 5). *Debt management* — the substrate makes both technical debt (code that no longer matches the architecture the team would design today) and cognitive debt (vocabulary that no longer matches the concepts the team would name today) visible and nameable; it does not pay either down, but it lets the team decide what to carry, refactor, or defer to the bridge period (Pattern 27).

**Refactored target state.** A precision matters here: the ontology is not a description of the legacy’s vocabulary, even after recovery. It is the **refactored target state** the modernization is moving the system toward. The recovery phase analyses the legacy’s drifted ontology to surface candidate terms and expose contradictions; the design phase resolves those contradictions and articulates the coherent language the modernized system will use. The legacy informs the language; the legacy does not constrain it.

**Independence and durability.** The substrate is not a mental model in the team’s heads. It is not a glossary in a wiki that no one updates. It is a queryable, versionable artifact, maintained on its own change cadence, consumed by every other pattern that needs canonical vocabulary. Once recovered, it lives on the rhythm of the *domain*, not the rhythm of the code: frameworks evolve, cloud architectures change, the team turns over, and the canonical vocabulary persists. It is the bridge between business strategy and technical architecture: the business reads it as the description of its own concepts, the architecture reads it as the contract every modernized component must respect. Nick Tune has documented how the target model itself drifts during migration — concepts that began as straightforward renames end up restructured as the team’s understanding sharpens (see *[Drifting Domain Model](#gl-drifting-domain-model)* in the glossary) — so the ontology is versioned and revisable, with the harness (Pattern 22) recording each revision in the modernization’s audit trail.

![*The ontology as diagnostic substrate: when a single legacy term resolves to two incompatible meanings across subdomains, the split is itself evidence of a bounded-context boundary. The ontology surfaces drift rather than hiding it.*](images/diagram-p4-ontology.png)

### Consequences

The modernization has a referent that is independent of the legacy. Disagreements within the legacy can be reconciled against the ontology rather than preserved by default. The vocabulary in the modernized C# matches the domain, not the historical accidents of how the legacy expressed the domain.

The substrate functions as cognitive infrastructure, not only code infrastructure. The team has a shared artifact against which design conversations, review comments, ontology disagreements, and refactoring proposals all resolve. *Behavioural Equivalence Without Ontology* — the antipattern this pattern defends against — becomes visible: when the modernization preserves three behaviours the ontology says should be one, the divergence is addressable rather than invisible. This is how the substrate becomes a tool for managing technical and cognitive debt — not by paying it down, but by making each discrepancy named, sized, and trackable, available for prioritisation alongside every other modernization investment. What it prevents is silent accumulation.

The cost is real. Ontology recovery requires domain experts whose time is scarce, reconciling sources that disagree, and judgement calls that are not derivable from the legacy. It also requires choosing between adopting industry taxonomies, adapting them, or building from scratch — a strategic-design decision (Pattern 1) with implications the organization has to live with. Most modernizations do not do this work, which is why most modernizations inherit the ontological drift of their predecessors.

The risk is not only that the work is skipped; it is that the work is done *wrong*. A misrecovered canonical term is high-blast-radius in exactly the way the substrate's value implies: the pattern asks every modernized component to respect the ontology as its contract, so an error in the canonical vocabulary propagates into every component that honours it. Resolve *balance* to the wrong one of its three meanings and the mistake is encoded uniformly across the modernized estate — cleaner to read than the legacy's drift, and harder to detect precisely because it is consistent. This is why the substrate is versioned and revisable rather than fixed at recovery: the canonical vocabulary is a hypothesis the modernization commits to provisionally and corrects as understanding sharpens, with each revision recorded in the harness (Pattern 22). An ontology treated as settled fact rather than revisable commitment is the dangerous case.

A precision worth stating: the substrate is necessary but not sufficient for ubiquitous language. Evans’ ubiquitous-language discipline is not a stored vocabulary file — it is the practice of the team *speaking* canonical vocabulary in conversations, in commit messages, in pull-request review, in incident response. The substrate makes the canonical vocabulary queryable and enforceable in code; it does not by itself make the team speak it. Confusing the artifact for the discipline is one of the more common DDD failures, and this pattern earns its claim only when both are in place. The patterns that support the discipline operationally are named in Related Patterns below.

### Related patterns

The *Behavioural Equivalence Without Ontology* antipattern names the failure mode this pattern protects against — without Pattern 4, modernization preserves the legacy’s confusion under the appearance of fidelity. Pattern 2 (*The Legacy as Oracle*) is what this pattern complements: behavioural fidelity is necessary but not sufficient, and Pattern 4 names the missing piece. Pattern 3 (*The Graph as Projection*) provides structural and semantic input that ontology recovery can draw on — vocabulary inference from comments and naming, similarity clustering from the semantic index — as starting points rather than substitutes. Pattern 8 (*The Intermediate Representation*) consumes the substrate when available — IR vocabulary aligns with canonical concepts rather than with whatever the legacy happened to use.

Three patterns support the ubiquitous-language discipline that the substrate alone cannot enforce. Pattern 21 (*Heuristics as Explicit Artifacts*) lets reviewer corrections about vocabulary feed back into the catalog as durable refinement. Pattern 23 (*The Control Plane*) surfaces ontology violations to architects during review. Pattern 24 (*Team Topology and Bounded Context Alignment*) names which team owns vocabulary discipline for each bounded context. The substrate is the artifact those patterns operate on.

Pattern 19 (*Bounded MCP Servers*) develops the published-language framing operationally — the canonical interface between deterministic infrastructure and probabilistic agents is itself a published language in Evans’ sense. The choice an organization makes between adopting an industry taxonomy as published language (BIAN, FIBO, ACORD, GS1) or authoring its own is a strategic-design decision (Pattern 1) the ontology substrate makes concrete and trackable.

A note on scope: bounded-context discovery — deciding where the legacy actually divides into modernization units — is a synthesis activity drawing on the capability map (Pattern 1), the structural clusters surfaced by community detection (Pattern 3), the vocabulary-boundary evidence this pattern provides, the slice signals of Pattern 5, and the operational [seam](#gl-seam) typology Ferri & Coggrave (2024) develop for mainframe-specific intervention points. The synthesis is currently distributed across these patterns rather than owned by one; this catalog treats decomposition as the joint work of all of these substrates, with Pattern 6 (*Context Map*) naming how the discovered contexts relate once they exist. The catalog will name seam discovery as its own pattern in a forthcoming revision; until then the lineage is acknowledged here.

-----

## Pattern 5: Slice and Seam Discovery from Structural and Behavioural Signals

### Context

A legacy codebase parsed as a graph and indexed for semantic similarity (Pattern 3). The graph contains thousands of paragraphs, hundreds of programs, dozens of [bounded contexts](#gl-bounded-context). The modernization needs to identify two related but distinct things from this substrate: **[vertical slices](#gl-vertical-slice)** — coherent feature units that can be extracted, scaffolded, translated, and verified as working wholes — and **[seams](#gl-seam)** — technical points where the modernization can intervene in the legacy without rewriting it.

Capability ⊃ bounded context ⊃ vertical slice ⊃ aggregate. The catalog uses these as nested concepts of different scope: a capability spans contexts, a bounded context contains many slices, a slice touches one or more [aggregates](#gl-aggregate) within a context. Each level is the unit of a different kind of work. Slice discovery is *tactical* work at the granularity below bounded context and above paragraph.

A *vertical slice* is one user-visible behaviour from input through processing through output, **including its side-effect tail** — the set of external resources it writes to, queues it dispatches onto, or external systems it calls. This catalog uses *slice* rather than DDD's *use case* because the side-effect surface encodes integration contracts the modernized side must preserve; naming the slice as a unit that explicitly includes its side effects is the more honest framing for mainframe modernization.

A *seam* is a point in the legacy where intervention is possible without rewriting the legacy itself — Michael Feathers' original framework for legacy code (Feathers, 2004), specialised for mainframes by Ferri & Coggrave (2024). Seams are the operational counterpart to slices: a slice is a unit of work the modernization will own; a seam is a point at which the legacy can hand work over, accept work back, or be observed in flight. Slice and seam discovery share signals and shape each other — what Ferri & Coggrave describe as the *symbiosis between technical seams and capability-driven increments*.

### Problem

Neither slices nor seams are stated explicitly anywhere in the legacy. Both must be inferred.

A bounded context is too large to be the unit of work; a paragraph is too small. The right unit is between — a slice that includes the side effects it produces (writes to DB2 or VSAM, messages onto MQ or CICS queues, calls to external systems, audit log entries, notifications dispatched), not just the logic that triggers them. A slice that excludes its side-effect tail is incomplete: extracting it would leave the side effects orphaned, attached to no one's modernized counterpart.

Seams are equally undeclared. A legacy that has run for decades has intervention points where the system already breaks open — file handoffs between batch steps, API entry points, database boundaries, transaction commits — but no document enumerates them, and the ones that look promising in source review are not always the ones that survive the *good-seam* test (Ferri & Coggrave 2024): observable interfaces, easily divertable traffic, usable by external (non-legacy) code, and a funnel-like surface area narrow enough that intervention is tractable.

Pure structural derivation produces candidates that are syntactically coherent but operationally meaningless: paragraphs that share data may belong to the same slice or may merely have been written by the same person. Pure observational derivation (which transactions actually run together) reflects current usage patterns but misses structural coherence: heavily-used transactions dominate, lightly-used ones disappear. Either signal alone is insufficient. For seams specifically, the failure mode is sharper — a seam that looks structurally clean but is not actually divertable in production wastes the modernization's most expensive resource.

### Forces

Slices and seams are not the same thing, but they are not independent either. A slice is a *semantic unit of value*: a feature, a capability fragment, a piece of business behaviour. A seam is a *technical intervention point*: a place where the modernization can attach, observe, divert, or replace. Some seams align cleanly with slice boundaries — a batch step that processes a coherent slice of claims is both a slice (the claim-processing feature) and a seam (the file-handoff to the next step). Others do not — a database boundary is a seam but does not cleanly correspond to any single slice; a slice may cross several seams or none.

The two discoveries share signals. Structural cohesion in the graph, linguistic cues left by the original programmer, the data layer's DDL, operational observation when available, and synthetic execution when it is not — these inform both slice and seam candidates, weighted differently for each. DDL evidence is heavier for slice boundaries (the data extent of the slice is part of its definition); operational evidence is heavier for seams (whether the intervention point is actually divertable depends on traffic patterns, not just structure).

The symbiosis is what makes the joint discovery worth the engineering. Ferri & Coggrave document it: technical seams and capability-driven increments shape each other across the engagement. A capability the business wants to migrate first will prefer seams that align with it; seams that prove tractable will pull the increment plan toward the capabilities they cover. Trying to find slices without considering seams produces migration units the modernization cannot actually intervene at; trying to find seams without considering slices produces intervention points that fragment business value.

Not every structurally clean intervention point is a working seam. Ferri & Coggrave's *good-seam* test — observable, divertable, usable by external code, and funnel-like — is what separates the two; a candidate that fails any criterion is not yet a seam however clean it looks in source. The Pattern section carries these four criteria as the evidence each seam candidate must score against.

### Pattern

Derive slice and seam candidates from a shared multi-signal pipeline. The pipeline produces *paired outputs* — slices and seams as related discoveries from the same evidence — with each output carrying its own provenance.

A note on vocabulary. By **signal** the catalog means a class of evidence the substrate carries about how the legacy is composed — what calls what, what is similar to what, what runs together, what writes to whom. By **pipeline** it means the deterministic process that collects each signal, weighs it against the others, and combines them into candidates the architect validates. The pipeline is plumbing; the signals are evidence; the candidates are the output. Each signal contributes independently, and disagreement between signals is itself informative — a slice candidate that is structurally clean but operationally silent reads differently from one all signals corroborate.

**Signal sources** — six contribute, each with its own engineering cost and confidence weight:

- **Structural cohesion in the graph.** Paragraph clusters with high internal cohesion (shared data, predicates, ancestry from a common entry point), low external coupling, and bounded **side-effect surface** — the set of external resources the cluster writes to, queues it dispatches onto, or external systems it calls. Community-detection algorithms (Louvain, Leiden, modularity-based methods) named in Pattern 3 are the standard mechanism; for discovery specifically, cut quality matters more than algorithm pedigree, and engagement-specific experience teaches which algorithm produces candidates an architect will recognise.
- **Semantic similarity from the substrate's index** (Pattern 3). Embedding-based clustering surfaces paragraphs that are *about* the same thing even when structural coupling is loose. Useful for slice discovery (semantic neighbours often belong to the same feature) and for cross-context seam discovery (a function implemented in two places under different names is a candidate for consolidating at a single seam).
- **Linguistic cues encoded by the original programmer.** `CALL` and `EXEC CICS LINK` mark structural coupling within a slice; `XCTL` typically marks transition between bounded contexts (and is therefore a strong seam candidate); `START TRANSID` marks a new transactional unit; `EXEC CICS WRITE TS QUEUE` and `EXEC CICS WRITE TD QUEUE` mark asynchronous communication points where slices can split and where seams naturally form; shared copybooks mark data coupling. Each construct is a decision the original programmer made about how the system is composed.
- **The data layer's DDL.** DB2 schemas, VSAM file definitions, DCLGEN copybooks. Field names in DDL frequently preserve domain vocabulary better than working-storage names. Foreign keys reveal relationships hidden by procedural code. Constraints capture domain invariants. Where DDL boundaries align with proposed slice boundaries, slice-confidence increases; where they cross, the divergence is informative. For seams, the data layer often *is* the seam — a database read or write boundary is one of Ferri & Coggrave's six internal seam types, and the DDL is what tells you whether the boundary is tractable.
- **Operational observation when available.** Paragraphs and transactions exercised together by real traffic, with shared inputs and shared outputs. For slices, this confirms or contradicts structural intuition; for seams, this is the primary evidence of divertability — traffic patterns reveal which intervention points carry the load and which are theoretical.
- **Synthetic execution when production telemetry is not available.** The Legacy Twin (Pattern 2), instrumented for paragraph-level execution tracing, produces equivalent signal under synthetic test inputs. The trace reveals which paragraphs activate together and where the system already breaks open across batch steps or transaction boundaries. The synthetic signal is not equivalent to production observation — it reflects test design, not real usage — but it catches many divergences between structural intuition and operational reality. For seam discovery specifically, synthetic execution is weaker evidence (divertability is a production property), but it is enough to enumerate candidates the architect then validates against operational signal when available.

The heuristics mapping signals to slice and seam candidates live as queryable artifacts (Pattern 21), not as implicit knowledge in agent prompts. Specialized agents query the catalog when they need to interpret a cue in context; when an agent proposes a candidate, it emits a *reasoning record* — a structured artifact recorded alongside the action, citing which heuristics from the catalog were applied with what weights, which alternatives were considered, and what substrate evidence supported the chosen interpretation. The reasoning record is the audit trail the harness (Pattern 22) operates on; it is not a freeform explanation, it is a typed record the platform can query.

For CICS specifically, pseudo-conversational boundaries (`RETURN TRANSID`/`COMMAREA` cycles) are strong structural evidence for both: they signal where one user-visible interaction ends (slice boundary) and where the system already accepts external intervention (seam candidate). Use this as the primary structural anchor for CICS-based modernizations.

**Slice output**: a set of candidates, each with an entry point, a set of participating paragraphs, a side-effect surface, an estimated **tier classification** (Pattern 9 — core / supporting / generic, which determines what scaffold architecture the slice will be rendered into: hexagonal for core, hybrid for supporting, vertical-slice for generic), a data-layer extent (which tables, which columns, which VSAM keys, which IMS hierarchy regions), and a confidence signal indicating signal agreement.

**Seam output**: a typology of intervention points across the legacy estate. Following Ferri & Coggrave (2024), seam candidates fall into characteristic types:

- *External seams*: **batch input** at the file-transfer boundary (Event Interception + Dual Run, Pattern 27); **API access** through a gateway proxy (Dark Launching, Canary Release).
- *Internal seams*: **data interactions** as a seam (CDC replication for readers, Extract Product Lines for writers); **database readers** specifically (CDC-fed cloud-side access); **database writers** specifically (Legacy Mimic adapters); **batch pipeline step handoff** between VSAM or flat-file boundaries; **data characteristic** (segmenting work by data attributes, simple cases first); **downstream processing handoff** for side effects (audit, billing) that must continue running on the mainframe.

Each seam candidate carries the four good-seam criteria as evidence: observable (how), divertable (by what mechanism), externally usable (through which protocol or technique), funnel-like (how narrow the surface). A candidate that fails any criterion is annotated rather than discarded — the failing criterion is what the architect must address before the seam becomes operational, and is itself an input to engagement planning.

The integration patterns each seam type uses — Event Interception, Legacy Mimic, Extract Product Lines, Transitional Architecture (Pattern 25), Dark Launching, Canary Release — are catalogued in Newman *et al.*'s *Patterns of Legacy Displacement* and operationalised in this catalog through Pattern 11 (commands and events), Pattern 27 (dual-run mechanics), and Pattern 28 (modern facade). Seam discovery surfaces the candidate; the integration pattern names how the seam is activated.

Slice and seam outputs cross-reference each other, and the relationship is sharper than it first appears: **the side-effect surface of a slice is its candidate seam set, viewed from outside**. The side-effect surface enumerates what the slice does to the world; the seam-set enumerates where the world can intervene on the slice. These are the same set of points described from the slice's interior and exterior respectively. A seam that aligns with a slice boundary therefore strengthens both. A slice that crosses an untractable seam is a planning warning — the migration unit will need to absorb work the team cannot easily intervene at. A seam without a clearly associated slice is a strategic option — the intervention point exists, but the value it unlocks needs articulation.

**Output shape.** Candidates are rendered through the catalog's emitters (Pattern 10) into views the architect actually reads: *slice maps* showing each candidate as a graph with participating paragraphs, side-effect surface, and DDL extent annotated; *seam-typology tables* listing each candidate with its Ferri & Coggrave type, its good-seam evidence scored, and its associated integration pattern; *dependency diagrams* overlaying both, showing where slices cross seams and where seams enclose multiple slices; and *markdown summaries* per candidate, with every claim linked back to the substrate query, the linguistic cues, and the operational traces that produced it. The control plane (Pattern 23) surfaces these views; one click moves from candidate to evidence to substrate query.

![*Slice and seam discovery from a shared multi-signal pipeline: structural, linguistic, behavioural, and data signals are weighed into paired candidates. A slice's side-effect surface is its candidate seam set, seen from the inside rather than the outside.*](images/diagram-p5-slices.png)

### Consequences

The modernization gets two paired discoveries from one analytical investment: units of work that are operationally meaningful (slices) and intervention points that are technically tractable (seams). The agents work on real features; architects validate slice and seam boundaries based on combined evidence — structural, semantic, linguistic, data-layer, operational, and synthetic. Each candidate carries provenance that traces back to the substrate elements that grounded it.

Slices are not the unit of business value — capabilities are. A business capability ("process insurance claim," "underwrite policy renewal," "reconcile end-of-day") typically composes multiple slices. Capability mapping (Pattern 1) and slice discovery answer different questions: capability mapping asks *what the business does*; slice discovery asks *how the legacy implements it*. The two should agree at the level of what aggregates into what — a coherent capability composed of incoherent slices is a discovery failure, and a coherent slice that does not aggregate into a recognisable capability is a planning failure. Neither replaces the other. The same applies to seams: a seam that does not eventually serve a capability migration is a theoretical intervention point, not an engagement asset.

A slice is not only a piece of code. It is also a slice of the data layer: a subset of database tables, sometimes a subset of the *columns* of those tables, sometimes a region of VSAM keys, sometimes a partition of an IMS hierarchy. Extracting a slice means refactoring both the code and the data simultaneously — and the data side is usually harder. Chris Richardson, writing about microservice extraction from monoliths, names this directly: enterprises tend to have schemas complex enough that no individual person holds them in their head, and the extraction work is dual-track refactoring of both code and schema (Richardson, *Microservices Patterns*, 2018; *Software Evolution with Microservices and LLMs*, InfoQ Podcast, 2026). The catalog's discovery pipeline reflects this: DDL evidence is one of the six signal sources, and the data-layer extent is a first-class part of both slice and seam definitions. Pattern 17 (*Data Drift Analysis*) and Pattern 27 (*Dual-Run Coexistence*) operate on what discovery declares about the data; if the data-layer extent is wrong, the verification and coexistence patterns inherit the error.

The cost is the multi-signal pipeline as ongoing infrastructure. Each signal carries its own engineering cost — graph queries built and maintained, [embedding](#gl-embedding) pipelines kept current, DDL parsers calibrated per legacy data layer, observation capture deployed and operated, synthetic execution traces generated and curated. Signal weights and fallback behaviour evolve across engagements: what was the right weight for one mainframe estate may not be right for the next, and the architect must understand the calibration well enough to defend it.

There is also a judgment cost. When one signal is unavailable — no production observation yet for a greenfield-side context, DDL not exhaustive for a legacy with VSAM-heavy data, synthetic execution producing too thin a trace — the architect must understand and accept the resulting confidence reduction, then decide whether to proceed or to invest in restoring the missing signal. Slice and seam candidates often do not match what the architect expected; reconciling the discrepancy is its own work, and the discipline of taking the evidence seriously when intuition disagrees is harder than the engineering.

### Related patterns

Pattern 3 (*The Graph as Projection*) provides the structural and semantic substrate this pipeline operates on. Pattern 4 (*Domain Ontology as Independent Substrate*) provides the canonical vocabulary slice boundaries align with. Pattern 7 (*The Compiler Principle*) is why the heuristic catalog lives as deterministic infrastructure. Pattern 9 (*Tier-Aware Scaffolding*) consumes slice candidates and produces the appropriate scaffold. Pattern 10 (*Pluggable Emitters*) renders slice and seam candidates into views the architect can validate. Pattern 15 (*Hypothesis-Driven Verification*) provides the behavioural signal that refines both discoveries. Pattern 19 (*Bounded MCP Servers*) is where specialized discovery agents live with explicit access to the heuristic catalog. Pattern 21 (*Heuristics as Explicit Artifacts*) holds the catalog this pattern consumes. Pattern 22 (*The Harness as Self-Observing State Machine*) makes heuristic application observable through its reasoning telemetry.

For seams specifically: Pattern 11 (*Commands and Events*) is where the Event Interception and Legacy Mimic integration patterns are operationalised at runtime. Pattern 25 (*Transitional Architecture*) is the modular-monolith pattern Ferri & Coggrave reference under the same name. Pattern 27 (*Dual-Run Coexistence*) operates the seams during the bridge period. Pattern 28 (*Replatform with Modern Facade*) is where facade-style seams (API gateways, modern adapters in front of legacy cores) live.

The vertical-slice framing comes from Bogard and Smith (2018). [Event Storming](#gl-event-storming) (Brandolini, 2013) is the workshop technique for collaborative slice discovery with domain experts. The seam concept originates with Michael Feathers (*Working Effectively with Legacy Code*, 2004); the mainframe-specific seam typology and the good-seam criteria are from Ferri & Coggrave's *Uncovering the Seams in Mainframes for Incremental Modernisation* (martinfowler.com, 2024). The integration patterns each seam uses are catalogued in Newman *et al.*'s *Patterns of Legacy Displacement* (martinfowler.com, ongoing); the programme-mobilisation framing for seam-driven approaches comes from Holden's *Eating the Elephant*. What this catalog adds is the codebase-scale machinery — linguistic cues, multi-signal pipeline, heuristic catalog, paired slice/seam outputs — applied to mainframes where the graph and the [semantic index](#gl-semantic-index) together carry most of the evidence the architect needs to validate a candidate, with Event Storming reserved for the human-side workshops that resolve what the substrate alone cannot.

-----

## Pattern 6: Context Map for Modernization

### Context

A modernization is a system in motion. At any point along its arc, some [bounded contexts](#gl-bounded-context) are still legacy, some are modernized, some are dual-running, some are being newly designed for the target state. The bounded contexts at the start of the engagement are not the bounded contexts at the end; the relationships between them are not the same either. Across this landscape, every pair of bounded contexts has a relationship: they share concepts, they integrate through messages, they ignore each other, they fight for ownership of overlapping domain ground. The relationships are everywhere, and they are not static.

In DDD vocabulary, the artifact that articulates these relationships is the **[context map](#gl-context-map)**. Eric Evans introduced the concept in *Domain-Driven Design* (2003) and named seven characteristic relationship types between bounded contexts: [shared kernel](#gl-shared-kernel), [customer-supplier](#gl-customer-supplier), [conformist](#gl-conformist), [anti-corruption layer](#gl-anti-corruption-layer), [separate ways](#gl-separate-ways), [open host service](#gl-open-host-service), and [published language](#gl-published-language). Vaughn Vernon elaborated their operational mechanics in *Implementing Domain-Driven Design* (2013). Nick Tune and Krisztina Hirth’s *Bounded Context Canvas* (DDD Crew, ongoing) is the most actively-maintained contemporary articulation — *most actively-maintained* meaning it is still revised in public, with an open-source canvas and worked examples updated as the practice evolves rather than frozen at publication. For a modernization team, that currency matters: it reflects how context mapping is done today, not only how it was first defined.

This catalog adapts the context map for the specific shape of modernization work — and the adaptation runs deeper than relabelling. It would be easy to take Evans' context map exactly as he defined it, swap the example boxes for COBOL program names, and call it a mainframe context map; that would change the labels but not the artifact. What this catalog does instead is give the context map properties Evans' version does not have. It is *temporal* — a sequence of states across the migration rather than a single snapshot, because relationships change as contexts transition through stages. It is *stacked* — its decisions have an internal dependency order, where subdomain identity constrains which relationship types are coherent, which in turn constrains which carry a published language. And it is *focused on the modernized business contexts together with the external vocabularies they must speak*. The Pattern section develops each property; the consequence of taking them together is the point worth stating up front: the context map becomes the place where the migration plan is *derivable* from strategic decisions rather than improvised under operational pressure once the modernization is already underway.

### Problem

In legacy modernization, cross-context relationships accumulate by default rather than by design. The default failure modes are predictable and all of them are *temporal* — they are relationships that should have changed but did not, or that changed without anyone naming the change.

*Inherited conformist relationships.* The modernized side accepts whatever vocabulary and semantics the not-yet-modernized legacy emits, because the alternative (translating at the boundary) costs engineering. Over time the modernized side becomes a *conformist* to the legacy — its domain model bends to fit the legacy’s **accidental decomposition**: the way the legacy happens to be divided into programs, files, and modules, which reflects forty years of expedient decisions, reorganisations, and individual programmers’ habits rather than any deliberate domain design. Conforming to that accidental shape means the modern system inherits boundaries no one would choose today. Evans is clear that conformist is sometimes appropriate (when the upstream is genuinely authoritative and immovable) and often a sign of strategic weakness (when the modernized side could have been protected by an anti-corruption layer but wasn’t).

*Big-ball-of-mud relationships within the modernized side.* A modernization can manufacture a fresh [big ball of mud](#gl-big-ball-of-mud) — a system whose architecture has eroded into everything-depends-on-everything — even while writing clean code, if the relationships *between* its new bounded contexts are left undesigned. As contexts proliferate, the temptation is to share concepts opportunistically — *“both bounded contexts need a Customer; let’s just import the same class.”* That shared class is shared kernel by accident rather than design. Shared kernels are a real Evans relationship type, but they require *team-level agreement* — a standing commitment that neither team changes the shared core without consulting the other — and without it the accidental kernel degrades into pollution as each side bends the concept toward its own needs. (*Big ball of mud*, *shared kernel*, and *team-level agreement* are defined in the Glossary.)

*Separate ways treated as failure.* Two bounded contexts with no useful integration are sometimes pressured into integrating because *not* integrating feels like architectural laziness. Evans names *separate ways* as a legitimate relationship type — when the cost of building and maintaining an integration exceeds the value it delivers, **the right answer is no integration at all**: the two contexts are left deliberately disconnected, each solving its own problem, with any overlap handled by humans or by duplicate data rather than by a maintained technical link. Refusing an integration is a real design decision, not an omission. Modernizations that suppress this option produce architectures where **every context talks to every other** — a fully-connected mesh in which each new context must be wired to all the existing ones — *regardless of whether the integration earns its keep*. The connection count grows with the square of the number of contexts; most carry little value while all carry maintenance cost. The mesh is how a modernization rebuilds a big ball of mud out of well-built parts.

*Anti-corruption layers that should be retired live forever.* An anti-corruption layer (ACL) is a translation boundary one context places between itself and another, so the other’s vocabulary never leaks into its own; in modernization its usual job is to protect the modernized side from the legacy’s drifted vocabulary (defined in the Glossary; runtime mechanism in Pattern 11, operational deployment in Pattern 27). An ACL is by design *transitional* — it exists because the upstream is the legacy, and the legacy is being retired. Without explicit articulation of the relationship as transitional with a retirement criterion, ACLs become permanent infrastructure that outlives the legacy they were protecting against. The strategic decision to retire the ACL is what this pattern names; the operational lifecycle is developed in Pattern 27.

### Forces

Cross-context relationships have different costs and different lifetimes. Some are *durable* — they will hold for the lifetime of the modernized system, and the engineering they require is amortised across that lifetime. Some are *transitional* — they exist only because of the modernization itself, and are designed to be retired. The two categories require different engineering and different governance.

Classical context maps capture *spatial* relationships: X relates to Y in this way. A modernization needs to capture *temporal sequences*: X relates to Y as conformist now, as anti-corruption layer at stage two, as separate ways at steady state. The spatial framing is necessary but insufficient. Each transitional relationship needs a retirement criterion — the evidence that will say *this relationship can now be removed* — without which transitional infrastructure becomes **permanent by attrition**: not through a decision to keep it, but through the erosion of the reasons to remove it. The people who built the ACL move on, the urgency that justified the bridge API fades, the legacy is never quite finished being retired, and one day no one remembers why the translation layer exists or whether it is safe to delete — so it stays, carrying maintenance cost no one can confidently end. A retirement criterion fixed at design time is the antidote: it records, while the reasoning is fresh, the condition under which the infrastructure has done its job and can go.

**Subdomain identity constrains which relationship types are coherent.** A subdomain is a problem-space construct (a part of the business — claims, billing, pricing); a bounded context is a solution-space construct (a linguistic scope within which a model and vocabulary apply consistently). DDD’s ideal is one-to-one alignment, but legacy systems rarely deliver it: one subdomain is often fragmented across several contexts with different vocabularies, or one context serves several subdomains (a "master file" holding customers, products, and policies at once). Pattern 4 establishes how subdomain identity is recovered — the ontology is organised by subdomain, so a concept carrying two incompatible definitions signals two subdomains, or one wrongly fused in the legacy. This pattern consumes that identity and turns it into a constraint on relationships. Two contexts serving fragments of the same subdomain almost always need *shared kernel* or *anti-corruption layer* and cannot rationally be *separate ways* — they are the same part of the business, and any published language between them must trace back to one ontology entry, not two. Two contexts serving distinct subdomains can often be *separate ways*; forcing them into *shared kernel* is usually wrong. Subdomain type (core, supporting, generic) comes from Pattern 1; subdomain identity is the first stack layer, and the relationship-type decision rests on it.

There is also a force of accumulation. Without explicit articulation, the relationship type for any given pair of bounded contexts is whatever the most recent integration happened to require. The *easy* relationship types — conformist, accidental shared kernel — accumulate because they cost nothing upfront and everything to undo. The *deliberate* relationship types — open host service, anti-corruption layer with retirement criterion — appear only where someone explicitly built them. A modernization without an explicit context map is one where **accumulation dominates regardless of fit** — meaning the relationships that end up in the system are the ones that were cheapest to add in the moment, not the ones that suit the domain. The architecture drifts toward whatever required the least effort at each integration point, and the sum of locally-cheap decisions is a globally-wrong structure. An explicit context map reverses the default: it forces each relationship to be chosen for fit and defended, so that *fit* dominates over *ease*.

A force specific to the mainframe sharpens all of the above: **the legacy enforced no boundaries, so the modernization must impose them rather than draw them.** Greenfield DDD draws boundaries into open space — you decide where Claims ends and Billing begins, and the code respects the line because you build it that way. A mainframe offers no such blank space. There is typically one CICS region, one shared set of VSAM files and DB2 tables, one address space in which any program can call any other and read any record. The bounded contexts a modernization recovers are *logical* — they exist in the analysis, not yet in the running system. Every boundary the context map names is therefore a boundary that does not currently exist and must be *constructed*: a shared file split into two, a copybook duplicated and allowed to diverge, a direct `CALL` replaced by a published interface. This inverts the usual cost model. In greenfield work the boundary is free and the integration costs; on the mainframe the integration already exists (everything is integrated with everything) and the *boundary* is what costs. The context map is, in this light, a list of separations the modernization has to pay for — which is exactly why naming them, typing them, and sequencing them matters so much more here than in a system that was built with boundaries from the start.

A final force concerns the edges of the map: the modernized business contexts integrate with external vocabularies the organization does not author and cannot negotiate (industry taxonomies, regulatory standards). These are easy to leave off the map because no one inside the organization owns them, yet omitting them produces ad-hoc, duplicated integrations where one shared, named relationship belonged. They are developed in the Pattern section as the map's durable anchors.

### Pattern

Treat the context map as a first-class strategic artifact of the modernization — versionable, queryable, maintained alongside the capability map (Pattern 1) and the ontology (Pattern 4) as part of the strategic-recovery substrate. For each pair of bounded contexts, at each transition stage, name the Evans relationship type, the *lifetime* (durable or transitional), and (if transitional) the *retirement criterion* — the evidence that will say the relationship can be removed.

**The context map is temporal.** The relationship type at the start of the modernization may differ from the type at steady state. A bounded context that begins as *conformist to legacy* may transition to *anti-corruption layer to legacy* (when the modernized side gains leverage), then to *separate ways* (after the legacy is retired). Each stage is a designed relationship, named explicitly, with the criterion for moving to the next stage made operational. Pattern 27 is where the transitional relationships are operationally exercised; the context map is where the strategic sequence those operations realise is named.

**Bounded-context boundaries are recovered from multiple signals, side-effect clustering among them.** The standard DDD test for a bounded context is linguistic: a context is where a single [ubiquitous language](#gl-ubiquitous-language) applies. In a mainframe whose language has drifted, that test alone is partial. A complementary signal carries the same information: slices that share side-effects belong together; slices that don't, don't. Two slices writing the same tables, dispatching onto the same queues, or calling the same external systems are operationally coupled regardless of shared vocabulary; two that share vocabulary but no side-effects are operationally independent. The side-effect graph from Pattern 5 is therefore boundary evidence in its own right, complementary to the vocabulary-boundary evidence from Pattern 4: a candidate context whose internal slices share side-effects mostly with each other is structurally plausible; one whose slices are tangled with slices outside it is probably the wrong boundary. The linguistic test can itself be made an agentic verification assessment. Because a bounded-context boundary *is* a linguistic boundary — the place where one ubiquitous language ends and another begins — the question "is this the right boundary?" becomes "does the vocabulary change where the boundary falls, and stay consistent where it doesn't?" An agent checks the terms used inside a candidate boundary against the ontology (Pattern 4) and sorts what it finds into three cases. A term may **stay coherent** throughout — *Policy* means the same thing everywhere inside the candidate — which is simply what a healthy context looks like. A term may **shift at the edge** — *Policy* means the insurance contract inside Underwriting but the billing schedule once you cross into Billing — and a meaning that flips exactly where the line was drawn *confirms* the line, because that flip is what a real boundary is made of. Or a term may **shift within** the candidate — *Policy* meaning two different things among slices that are all inside the one proposed boundary — which *contradicts* it, signalling that what was drawn as one context is really two. The agent does not decide; it surfaces the three buckets with the specific terms attached, and the architect rules. The full assessment is developed in Pattern 15; the context map consumes its result as one more boundary signal.

**The map covers the modernized business contexts and their external-vocabulary relationships.** The primary subject is the modernized business contexts — Claims, Underwriting, Billing, Pricing, Customer Master, and whatever else the legacy decomposed into. This is where the transitional-vs-durable distinction is sharpest: most relationships *between* the legacy and modernized versions of the same business context are transitional and retire at cutover.

The map's edges, though, reach beyond the business contexts to the **external vocabularies** the system must speak: industry classification systems (NAICS, BIAN, FIBO, ACORD, GS1), regulatory taxonomies, the ISO message standards. These are not contexts the modernization builds or controls — in Pattern 4's strategy they are the *adopted* published languages of generic subdomains, maintained by standards bodies the organization can only conform to. They earn a place on the context map for two reasons. First, the relationship type is real and worth naming: a business context that consumes ACORD is in a *Published Language* relationship with it, and usually a *Conformist* one, since the organization does not get a vote on the standard. Second, these relationships are the map's **durable anchors** — while almost everything else on a modernization context map is transitional and designed to retire, a relationship to an industry standard does not retire at cutover; it is a fixed star in a moving sky. Naming them keeps the integrations governed (one shared mapping rather than many ad-hoc ones) and marks clearly which parts of the map are stable while the rest is in motion.

**Cross-context [seams](#gl-seam) are also published-language enforcement points.** Pattern 5 names the technical seams at boundaries; Pattern 6 names the strategic relationship those seams carry; the two views compose. A seam that crosses a bounded-context boundary is not just an intervention point — it is the place where the published language between those contexts is operationally enforced. The four good-seam criteria from Pattern 5 (observable, divertable, externally usable, funnel-like) are simultaneously the criteria for a usable published-language interchange: a seam that fails any of them cannot carry a published language reliably. Within-context seams (instrumentation, traffic mirroring, internal divert) carry traffic but not semantics; cross-context seams carry both, and the published language is what gives the semantic crossing its integrity. The context map names which relationships carry published language; the seam typology names the points at which those published languages cross.

**Engage all of Evans' relationship types — and recognise them by their mainframe signatures.** On a mainframe these relationship types are not abstractions to be assigned; they are already present in the legacy, in concrete physical form. Each type below is named by the legacy signature that reveals it:

- **Shared kernel** (a shared, agreement-maintained core) shows up as the **shared copybook** — usually shared by accident, not agreement, which makes it the most dangerous kind. Two programs that `COPY` the same `CUSTOMER-RECORD` layout share a data model by inclusion, not contract. When they become separate contexts, the copybook is the first thing to resolve: promote it to a governed shared kernel, translate it through an anti-corruption layer, or duplicate-and-diverge. The choice is a context-map decision; the copybook is where it bites.
- **Customer-supplier** is, before it is anything technical, an *organizational* relationship: the upstream (supplier) team agrees to let the downstream (customer) team's needs shape its priorities and backlog. That agreement is the whole pattern — without an upstream willing to negotiate its outputs, the downstream is not a customer but a *conformist*, taking whatever it is given. Its mainframe signature is the **synchronous inter-transaction call** — one CICS transaction invoking another through `EXEC CICS LINK` or `START`, the caller depending on the callee's behaviour and data layout. The technical link is easy to see; the organizational reality is the constraint that bites. The team that owns the called program controls a contract the caller depends on, and in a forty-year-old estate that team may no longer exist, may sit in a different division, or may have no mandate to change anything for a downstream consumer's benefit. The canonical modernization case is the modernized side as customer of a still-legacy supplier during dual-run — and its hardest part is rarely the call itself but securing the standing commitment that the legacy-owning team will honour the modernized side's needs while the legacy still runs. Where that commitment cannot be obtained, the relationship is not customer-supplier however it looks in the code, and the modernized side must protect itself with an anti-corruption layer instead.
- **Conformist** (one side accepts the upstream's vocabulary wholesale) is rare by choice in good modernization — usually a missing anti-corruption layer — with one honest exception: conformance to an external standard the organization cannot negotiate.
- **Anti-corruption layer** (translate at the boundary because you can neither accept nor influence the upstream) is *transitional* by default, retiring with the legacy; occasionally *durable* against a permanent external system. During dual-run its concrete form is often a **flat-file exchange or IBM MQ message**, whose retirement criterion is literal: the file or queue is decommissioned when the legacy side is gone (Pattern 27).
- **Separate ways** (no integration that earns its keep) is legitimate; on the mainframe the trap is mistaking *co-scheduled* for *coupled* — two jobs in one nightly chain may share nothing but a clock.
- **Open host service** (one published interface for all consumers; defined in the Glossary) is what the modernized side builds to replace the undocumented point-to-point integration the legacy never articulated as a service.
- **Published language** (a stable, documented shared vocabulary) has its most common undeclared mainframe instance in the **CICS COMMAREA or channel** — the byte layout passed between transactions, fixed positions and `REDEFINES` and filler, *is* the inter-context contract; recovering and naming it is "published languages already exist, undeclared" made concrete (Pattern 4). The ontology anchors these languages, commands and events carry them at runtime (Pattern 11), and agents can continuously police the match between emitted vocabulary and intended published language — flagging drift as risk, unrecognised shared vocabulary as opportunity (developed in Pattern 22, enforced at Pattern 19's MCP-server boundaries).

The single hardest boundary cuts across all these types: the **shared VSAM file or DB2 table**. Shared state is the strongest coupling there is — the mainframe's every-context-mesh made physical. Drawing a boundary through it forces the central tradeoff with no cheap option: split the store (expensive, risky, usually staged through Pattern 27's dual-run), accept a transitional shared kernel over the data, or route one side through an anti-corruption layer until the split is affordable. That there is no cheap option is exactly why the relationship must be named and sequenced, not left implicit.

The relationship types are Evans'; the signatures are the mainframe's. Recognising them is what turns context mapping from a whiteboard exercise into a reading of the actual system.

![*The context map is temporal: the relationship between two contexts is a designed sequence of stages — conformist, then anti-corruption layer, then separate ways — each with the retirement criterion that licenses the next. Coexistence is built to be dismantled.*](images/diagram-p6-contextmap.png)

### Consequences

Cross-context relationships become first-class architectural decisions, named and defended in review. The transition has a strategic spine, not just an operational one. When a relationship goes wrong in production, the team can trace the failure back to a specific design decision rather than to accumulated drift.

Conformist relationships become visible. When the modernized side is silently bending to fit the legacy's accidental decomposition, the context map shows it. The team can decide whether to accept the conformity (sometimes correct), invest in an anti-corruption layer (often correct), or pursue separate ways (occasionally correct). The choice is explicit.

The map separates what moves from what holds. Because every relationship carries a lifetime, the context map makes visible which parts of the architecture are transitional — the ACLs, bridge APIs, and conformist accommodations that should retire as the legacy is dismantled — and which are durable, chiefly the relationships to external vocabularies the business will speak indefinitely. A team reading the map can see, at any point in the transition, which integrations are scaffolding and which are permanent structure.

**The context map is readable as a migration plan.** Each Evans relationship type implies a specific operational pattern, stable enough to derive: *Customer-Supplier* with a still-legacy supplier implies a bridge API and dual-run infrastructure (Pattern 27); *Anti-Corruption Layer* implies the ACL lifecycle (Pattern 27 — discovery, defensive, bridging, authority-shift, decommission); *Published Language* implies stable schemas and the commands/events runtime (Pattern 11); *Open Host Service* implies a published API and often a modern facade (Pattern 28); *Shared Kernel* implies cross-team coordination (Pattern 24); *Separate Ways* implies no integration cost and is a decision to defend; *Conformist* implies absent protection and is usually a backlog item, not a steady state. Sequencing the migration is therefore largely *sequencing the transitional relationships* — which ACLs come first, which bridge APIs are built, which contexts retire in which order. The capability strategy (Pattern 1) sets which contexts matter most; the context map names what each transition requires.

The context map makes transitional architecture manageable over time. Each transitional relationship — an ACL, a bridge API, a conformist accommodation — is a piece of named, scoped, time-bounded debt, recorded with the condition for its own removal; Pattern 27 operationalises the lifecycle the map names strategically. Instead of transitional architecture being an undifferentiated mass of "temporary" infrastructure that quietly becomes permanent, it is an itemised ledger: this relationship retires when the legacy Claims module is decommissioned; that one is durable and stays. The map turns the transition from a fog into a backlog, letting a team answer at any point the two questions transitional architecture usually cannot: *how much of what we have built is temporary, and what has to become true for each piece of it to go away?*

The cost is genuine strategic work. The context map is not a side effect of writing integration code; it is a design exercise that happens separately, and it has to be maintained as the modernization progresses through its stages. The capability map (Pattern 1), the ontology (Pattern 4), the slice and seam discovery (Pattern 5), and the context map together carry the strategic spine of the modernization — each is its own artifact with its own discipline, each consumes substrates the prior patterns establish.

### Related patterns

Pattern 1 (*Business-Aligned Capability Strategy*) determines which capabilities exist and how they are treated; subdomain types from Pattern 1 constrain which relationship types are coherent in this pattern's map, and the capability strategy sets the sequencing priority for which transitional relationships are retired first. Pattern 4 (*Domain Ontology as Independent Substrate*) anchors the canonical vocabulary that published-language relationships use. Pattern 5 (*Slice and Seam Discovery*) is the technical counterpart at the boundary altitude — where the context map names *what kind of relationship* a context pair has, Pattern 5 names *where to operate* on that relationship; cross-context seams are also the published-language enforcement points the context map identifies. Pattern 11 (*Commands and Events as the Logical Boundary*) is the implementation mechanism for published-language relationships specifically. Pattern 19 (*Bounded MCP Servers*) operationalises the published-language discipline at runtime — the canonical interface a server exposes is a published language the context map can name. Pattern 24 (*Team Topology and Bounded Context Alignment*) is the organisational counterpart — customer-supplier relationships in the context map should align with team relationships in the topology, and where they do not, the misalignment is itself a design concern. Pattern 27 (*Dual-Run Coexistence*) is where the transitional-relationship discipline is operationally exercised; bridge APIs are anti-corruption layers in the context-map sense, and the ACL lifecycle Pattern 27 develops realises operationally what this pattern names strategically. Pattern 28 (*Replatform with Modern Facade*) is the operational pattern for relationships the context map types as open host service to an external consumer.

Eric Evans’ original treatment (*Domain-Driven Design*, 2003), Vaughn Vernon’s operational elaboration (*Implementing Domain-Driven Design*, 2013), and Nick Tune and Krisztina Hirth’s *Bounded Context Canvas* (DDD Crew, ongoing) are the lineage this pattern stands on. What this catalog contributes is the *stacked* framing — subdomain identity constrains coherent relationship types, relationship types constrain which carry published language, published-language relationships are enforced at cross-context seams, and each relationship type implies a specific operational pattern — composed into a single temporally-versioned artifact that maps the modernized business contexts together with their durable relationships to external vocabularies, and is readable as a migration plan.

-----

# Part II: Tactical Generation

-----

![](images/plate-iii-compiler.png)

-----

The patterns in this group address [tactical design](#gl-tactical-design) as DDD uses the term: how each bounded context materialises in modern code. They presuppose strategic recovery from Part I — without bounded contexts identified and ubiquitous language established, generation produces structurally plausible code that doesn’t reflect the domain.

The part has two movements. The first is *generation* — how [aggregates](#gl-aggregate), [domain events](#gl-domain-event), and handlers are produced, and how the architecture for each context is chosen and rendered. The compiler principle (Pattern 7) divides the deterministic work from the probabilistic; the intermediate representation (Pattern 8) is the contract between them; tier-aware scaffolding (Pattern 9) picks the architecture each context deserves; pluggable emitters (Pattern 10) render it. These patterns are calibrated to AI-assisted generation: they specify what humans decide, what agents produce, what deterministic infrastructure renders, and how the boundaries between them stay clean.

The second movement addresses *what decomposition introduces*. Splitting a co-located legacy into separately-deployed bounded contexts is not free — it replaces in-process calls with a logical boundary that must be made explicit (Pattern 11, *Commands and Events as the Logical Boundary*), it puts at risk the transactional guarantees the legacy enforced implicitly (Pattern 12, *Transactional Boundaries as First-Class Migration Concern*), and it introduces a network where there was none, with all the latency and unreliability that implies (Pattern 13, *Distribution Introduces a Network*). Generation produces the code; these three patterns ensure the distributed system it runs in preserves what the single address space gave for free.

-----

## Pattern 7: The Compiler Principle

### Context

An agentic system for software engineering, where large language models participate in code generation. Every such system contains two kinds of work, and they have different natures. Some decisions are *deterministic*: what scaffold to render, which [bounded contexts](#gl-bounded-context) exist, what types to declare, where a boundary falls. Given the same inputs, they should produce the same output every time. Other decisions are *probabilistic*: how to translate an idiomatic COBOL paragraph into idiomatic C#, how to handle an edge case the specification never mentioned, how to phrase a method that has no single right form. Given the same inputs, a language model may answer differently from one run to the next — and that variability is not a defect to be suppressed but the very capability that makes the model useful for the work no deterministic tool can do.

The question this pattern answers is not *how do we make the model deterministic* — that is the wrong goal, and pursuing it wastes the model's actual value. The question is *where does each kind of work belong*, and *what holds the boundary between them*. Get that wrong and the system is opaque, unreproducible, and impossible to audit: a change in a prompt silently alters an architectural decision, a regenerated file comes back subtly different, and no one can say afterwards what the system actually did or why. Get it right and the system is reproducible where it must be and creative where it can afford to be — with a hard, inspectable line between the two.

This is no longer a lonely position. By 2026 the practitioner and research communities had converged on the same separation from several directions, often under the banner of *LLMs as compilers, not interpreters* — generate once into a fixed artifact rather than re-deciding probabilistically on every execution. A few lessons recur, independent of the tools that demonstrate them. *Economically*, when generation is paid for once at compile time and execution cost falls to near zero, the compile-once-run-many shape becomes the rational default for well-specified, high-volume work — and sharpens as inference comes to dominate budgets. *Architecturally*, the durable way to constrain a model is to route its output through a representation that can be automatically validated against a specification, rather than trusting generated text directly. And most bluntly, learned by everyone who has shipped a production agent: determinism is imposed with contracts around the model — schemas, bounded retries, validation gates — not extracted from generation. Real determinism does not come from generation; it comes from acceptance. In regulated industries this stops being a preference and becomes a compliance line — the difference between being able to answer "what did the system actually do?" and not.

One further finding matters for what follows: the structure around the model often matters more than the model itself. On contemporary agentic-coding benchmarks the *same* model can swing thirty to fifty percentage points depending on the harness that wraps it — the tool set, the retry policy, the constraints it runs under. The reliability of an AI modernization system is a property of its *architecture*, not of the model plugged into it. How the two kinds of work are organised — what is compiled, what is generated, and what enforces the line — is what determines whether the system is reproducible and auditable, or opaque and unreliable.

### Problem

When the model is asked to make decisions of both kinds, the system becomes opaque and unreliable. Architectural choices made by the model aren't reproducible: ask twice, get different scaffolds. Boundary placements drift. Structural commitments are made on shaky ground.
