The deterministic infrastructure — compilers, linters, type systems — ends up checking outputs from a process that should never have produced them in that form.

The instinct to fix this through prompt engineering — telling the model which kinds of decision to make and which to defer — fails as the work scales. Prompts are advisory; the model treats them as suggestions, not contracts. A constraint that lives only in a prompt is not a constraint. The same five bounded contexts named in a prompt can come back as four on the next run; the same five encoded as nodes in the substrate the renderer reads cannot — the difference between advice and architecture.

Underneath this lies a sharper distinction, and getting it wrong is the deeper failure. Determinism can be imposed two ways. *By construction* — the model is given a grammar, a syntax-tree API, or a type schema it physically cannot violate, so the illegal output never comes to exist. Or *by rejection* — the model generates freely and a gate downstream accepts or rejects what it produced. Construction prevents; rejection catches. Prevention is total but rigid (the constraint must be specified in advance); rejection is flexible but pays for discarded work and catches only what the gate already knows to look for. A system that rejects where it could have constructed pays repeatedly for errors it could have made impossible. Knowing which discipline belongs where is the problem this pattern exists to solve — and the answer is not uniform across the system.

The hardest instance sits on the boundary itself: the strategic architectural decisions, and the type schema that encodes them. Which bounded contexts exist, where each boundary falls, what the [aggregate](#gl-aggregate) shapes are — these must be reproducible, because every downstream generation depends on them, yet they also require genuine judgement, the kind agents can *inform* but should not *make*. A decision that is neither freely the model's nor purely mechanical needs a discipline that is neither "let the model decide" nor "write a rule": that discipline is the subject of the Pattern below.

### Forces

Models are good at language transformation, idiom translation, and contextual disambiguation. They are bad at structural commitment, architectural integrity, and reproducible decision-making. Deterministic tools are the inverse. Both kinds of work are necessary in modernization, and mixing them in a single agent surface produces the worst of both: structure that drifts and translation that is over-constrained.

The division is not symmetric across the layers, and the pattern is sharper for saying where each discipline applies. *Structure* — the bounded-context inventory, the boundary placements, the aggregate shapes, the architectural style of the scaffold — is settled by human judgement and frozen into the substrate; the model never decides it, though agents inform it. *Type richness* — whether a field is a bare string or a validated value object, whether an invariant is encoded in the type system or checked at run time — is, wherever the ontology already carries the semantics, *also* a deterministic projection: if the recovered ontology (Pattern 4) knows that *Money* is a constrained quantity and that a *PolicyNumber* has a format, then the rich type is rendered from that knowledge, not invented per file. The model proposes type richness only in the genuine residual gap — where the ontology is silent and a human has not yet ruled — and even then the proposal is gated and, once accepted, folded back into the ontology so the next rendering derives it deterministically. The schema is not a place the model freely decides; it is a place the model occasionally *proposes into*, and every accepted proposal becomes deterministic substrate for the future.

A reasonable objection: models are improving fast, and what is true today about their structural reasoning may not hold in two years. If the next generation can make architectural decisions reproducibly, why not let it? Even then, three properties of the deterministic side survive. *Auditability*: regulated industries must inspect decisions independently of the process that produced them, which is cheaper against a deterministic substrate than by tracing a model's reasoning. *Replaceability*: when the model changes, deterministic decisions are unaffected while probabilistic ones need re-validation. *Contract clarity*: the deterministic side is the contract the probabilistic side must respect, and merging them makes that contract implicit and disagreements hard to triage. Better models change what agents can be entrusted to *propose* inside the substrate; they do not eliminate the substrate, nor the human acceptance that freezes proposals into it.

### Pattern

Separate deterministic decisions from probabilistic ones at the architectural level, not at the prompt level. Put deterministic decisions in deterministic infrastructure: the graph holds the architectural decisions, the architect validates them — with agents assisting the analysis but not making the call — and a deterministic emitter renders target-language scaffolds from the validated, settled decisions (in the reference implementation this is a Roslyn-based C# emitter; the principle holds for any language whose tooling can construct code structurally rather than as text — Pattern 8). Put probabilistic work inside the rendered scaffold: agents translate paragraph-level legacy logic into method bodies inside handler classes whose shape is already determined.

The agent does not choose architecture. The architecture is rendered from settled decisions, and the agent fills the constrained space.

That phrase — *the constrained space* — deserves to be made precise, because the line between what is compiled and what is generated falls in a less obvious place than "architecture is fixed, the method body is free." Three tiers, not two. The **architecture** is deterministically fixed: it is clearly defined in the graph, and the scaffold renders it the same way every time. But a second tier, which looks like generated code, is in fact also compilable: the **idiomatic and structural constraints of each unit** — method signatures, interface contracts, the variable names carried over from the legacy, and above all the *side-effect surface* (the read-set and write-set: which tables and files the unit reads, which it writes, which queues it dispatches onto — Pattern 5). These are *inferred from the original code*, but inference is not the same as judgement: a paragraph's read-set and write-set can be recovered by static analysis, and once recovered they are facts, not opinions. They can therefore be enforced as a *typed schema* and rendered into the scaffold deterministically (the IR-Scaffold of Pattern 8), rather than left to the model to reproduce and possibly get wrong. The generated method does not merely *aim* to honour the legacy's read/write behaviour — its signature and types make a method that reads or writes the wrong resource fail to compile.

What is left, after architecture and structural constraints are both compiled, is narrower than it first appears — and it is the part that genuinely needs a language model. The **business-logic algorithm** inside the unit: the actual computation a paragraph performs, inferred from the original COBOL but expressible in many correct C# forms, with no schema that can capture it because the algorithm *is* the content. This is the irreducibly probabilistic tier. The model infers it from the legacy and writes it into a method whose signature, types, and read/write sets are already fixed — generating an algorithm into a constrained shell, not writing a method freely. The compiler principle, stated at this resolution, is: *push as much as possible from the third tier into the second.* Every structural constraint that can be turned into an enforced schema is one less thing the probabilistic tier can get wrong — and what cannot be schematised, the business logic itself, is exactly where the model earns its place.

This resolves the knife-edge the Problem posed, as a four-step division of labour: **agents inform, the human decides, the substrate freezes, the compiler renders.** Agents run the analysis no human could do at scale and surface proposals with evidence; the architect applies judgement at an acceptance gate; the approved decision becomes a queryable fact in the graph rather than a sentence in a prompt — which is what makes it a contract; and the emitter renders it deterministically. Determinism here is a property of the *frozen result*, not of the deciding: the deciding is human judgement on an agent's proposal, and neither of those is deterministic. The freeze is what earns the word. The model's freedom begins only after all four steps, inside the space the scaffold leaves open.

The deterministic side is itself made of explicit artifacts: the graph schema is queryable, the IR types are inspectable, the scaffold rendering rules are code rather than prompt content. Even the heuristics the pipeline applies — tier classification (Pattern 9), slice-boundary proposals (Pattern 5), [anti-corruption layer](#gl-anti-corruption-layer) detection — live as first-class queryable catalogs (Pattern 21), not as knowledge baked into prompts. Determinism doesn't end at "the compiler exists"; it extends through every rule the compiler applies.

In DDD terms, the line between the compiled tiers and the generated one is the line between [strategic design](#gl-strategic-design) (performed by the human architect, with agent assistance for analysis) and tactical execution (performed by agents, within the scaffold strategic decisions produced).

One implication of this division deserves naming, because it is the deepest reading of the principle. The scaffold the compiler renders is not just *constraint* — at its strongest it makes whole classes of error impossible to express rather than merely caught. This works at three depths. Constraining generation to a grammar or schema makes malformed *output form* unrepresentable. Generating through a syntax tree rather than a string — Roslyn's `SyntaxFactory` rather than templated text — makes malformed *syntax* unrepresentable, because an invalid node cannot be constructed. And a type schema that encodes domain invariants makes illegal *domain states* unrepresentable: a `Money` that cannot be negative, a `PolicyNumber` that cannot hold an unvalidated string. These are three layers of the same discipline — form, syntax, semantics — each foreclosing a class of error by structure rather than by inspection, and together they are why a well-designed scaffold lets the agent's work hold across model generations: the conceptual model lives in the code, not the prompt. Pattern 8 develops this further.

![*The compiler principle, structurally: graph → IR → scaffold renders deterministically; agents fill the constrained space. The scaffold crosses the boundary; the decisions behind it do not.*](images/diagram-compiler.png)

### Consequences

The system becomes reproducible at the architectural level. The same graph produces the same scaffold every time. The same scaffold constrains agents to the same kinds of work. Failures are localised: when something goes wrong, it's clear whether the failure is in the architectural decision (wrong bounded context) or the agentic translation (wrong handler body). Each can be debugged independently. For regulated industries this matters operationally — auditors can review architectural decisions separately from generated code, and the audit trail at each layer is independently inspectable.

The cost is engineering discipline. The deterministic infrastructure has to actually be deterministic, which means a real compiler/emitter (Roslyn `SyntaxFactory`) rather than templated string interpolation. The graph has to actually hold the decisions, which means a schema rich enough to express them. The boundary between deterministic and probabilistic work has to be defended actively, and the acceptance gate that freezes proposals into the substrate has to be a real gate, not a rubber stamp.

The principle isn't novel in absolute terms — compilers have always been deterministic infrastructure with creative work happening inside their constraints, and the lineage of rule-driven generative systems runs back through Backus-Naur Form to Pāṇini's *Aṣṭādhyāyī* (c. 5th century BCE). What is new is not the discipline of separating rule-driven systems from open-ended work — that is ancient — but applying it to the architecture of agentic workflows: agents as the creative work, deterministic infrastructure as the harness around them. The field has not yet named this division as a principle in its own right. Although Rosetta operates in mainframe modernization specifically, the principle applies wherever AI-assisted code generation must coexist with architectural integrity.

A note on prior automation. Mainframe modernization has seen waves of automated tooling — rule-based translation engines, COBOL-to-Java converters, model-driven transformation suites. Most produced *Jobol*: code that is technically modern in syntax but inherits the legacy's structure, idioms, and architectural commitments. Some produced unmaintainable output that the customer is still paying to maintain a decade later. A practitioner reading this catalog is right to be sceptical about whether AI-era automation is meaningfully different from what came before. The argument here is not that LLMs generate code more skilfully than rule-based engines did — they sometimes do and sometimes don't. The argument is that AI assistance enables a *verification economy* that prior automation could not: millisecond-feedback loops against a live oracle (Pattern 14), production-mode hypothesis testing (Pattern 15), self-observing harness gates (Pattern 22), agent reasoning that can be inspected and refined (Pattern 21). Prior tools generated code; the verification loop afterwards was manual, slow, and rarely systematic. The economics of the verification loop are what this catalog argues have changed. The discipline that determines whether the modernization succeeds — strategic design done well, [tactical design](#gl-tactical-design) grounded in ontology, verification graded against ground truth — is the same as it was before agents existed. The compiler principle is what keeps that discipline applicable at the new speed.

### Related patterns

Patterns 7, 8, and 9 form the catalog's *deterministic-generation cluster*, naming a compiler, the language it consumes, and the policy that drives it: this pattern is the **compiler principle** (where the boundary between deterministic and probabilistic work falls and what enforces it), Pattern 8 (*The Intermediate Representation*) is the **DSL and semantic model** the compiler consumes — the typed language of architectural intent — and Pattern 9 (*Tier-Aware Scaffolding*) is the **policy** deciding which architecture the compiler renders per subdomain tier. They are read together but remain distinct: each can be done well or badly independently. Pattern 10 (*Pluggable Emitters*) is a corollary — if the principle holds, the deterministic side should be replaceable without disturbing the probabilistic side. Pattern 4 (*Domain Ontology as Independent Substrate*) is what makes type richness a deterministic projection rather than a model decision. The *Jobol* antipattern names what happens when the compiler principle is abandoned and mechanical COBOL-to-C# translation produces code that retains COBOL's idioms in C# syntax.

-----

## Pattern 8: The Intermediate Representation

### Context

An AI-assisted modernization pipeline has two halves with opposite temperaments. On one side is a flexible substrate where analysis happens — the property graph (Pattern 3), where [bounded contexts](#gl-bounded-context) emerge, where vocabulary is still being inferred, where uncertainty is the normal state and a finding may be revised as more of the legacy is understood. On the other side is a strict surface where generation happens — the emitter, where target-language code is produced and every field must be present, typed, and in exactly the right shape or nothing renders at all. Analysis tolerates ambiguity; generation tolerates none.

These two halves have to communicate, and the temptation is to wire them together directly: let the emitter read the graph, or let the analysis produce code-shaped output. Both couplings are a trap. Wire the emitter to the graph and every change to how analysis works forces a change to how code is rendered; wire the analysis to the emitter and the analysis can no longer evolve without breaking generation. The two halves move at different speeds — analysis evolves as the field learns what to look for in legacy code, generation stabilises around what compiles — and coupling them forces the faster half to wait for the slower and the slower half to churn with the faster. What is missing is a contract: a typed surface that lets each side change on its own clock as long as both honour the surface between them. That contract is the subject of this pattern.

### Problem

Without a typed contract between analysis and generation, every change to either side propagates everywhere, and the pipeline stiffens until it is cheaper to start over than to extend.

The symptoms are concrete. A new pattern discovered in analysis — say, a new kind of [saga](#gl-saga) the legacy turns out to use — requires touching the emitter directly to render it, because there is no intermediate vocabulary in which to *name* the new pattern before rendering it. A new architectural target on the generation side — rendering a bounded context as [hexagonal](#gl-hexagonal-architecture) rather than vertical-slice — requires re-deriving structure from the graph, because the architectural decision was never captured anywhere durable; it lived implicitly in the emitter's code. Two runs of the same analysis can produce different generated structure, because nothing pins down what was decided between them. And when something is wrong in the output, there is no layer at which to ask *was this a bad architectural decision or a bad rendering?* — the two are tangled in a single step.

Underlying all of these is one missing thing: a place to write down *what the modernization has decided* that is separate from both *how it was discovered* and *how it will be rendered*. The graph holds evidence; the code holds the result; nothing in between holds the **decisions** — and decisions are exactly what must be stable, inspectable, and reproducible. Worse, the naive fix of a single intermediate format conflates two genuinely different things: the architectural intent (which [aggregates](#gl-aggregate) exist, what events they emit) and the structural blueprint (which files, which class layouts). These have different audiences, different change rates, and different rules about who may modify them — and a single undifferentiated IR forces them to share a fate they should not share.

### Forces

Analysis must remain flexible, because the field is still discovering what to look for in legacy code and a recovery technique that cannot evolve is obsolete on arrival. Generation must remain strict, because rendering requires every piece of information in a specific shape and a missing field is a failed build, not a degraded one. Both pressures are real and pull in opposite directions; a single representation that tried to serve both would have to be simultaneously fluid and rigid.

A second tension runs through the intermediate itself: its vocabulary must be *stable* enough that downstream emitters can depend on it across engagements and model generations, yet *evolvable* enough that newly-discovered analysis patterns can extend it. Treat the IR as frozen and it cannot grow with what recovery learns; treat it as fluid and every analysis change breaks the tools that depend on it. Both extremes fail, and the resolution is not a compromise between them but a *split*: a layer that evolves with analysis and a layer that stabilises for generation, with a deterministic projection between.

A third tension is about authorship. Some of what the IR holds is genuine architectural judgement that must be validated by a human and then held immutable; some of it is mechanical structure that follows with no decisions left to make. If both live in one undifferentiated representation, there is no way to say *this part agents may propose into, this part is frozen* — and the most damaging failure of an AI modernization pipeline is precisely an agent quietly revising an architectural commitment because the representation gave it no reason not to.

### Pattern

Place a typed intermediate representation between the graph and the generated code. The graph (Pattern 3) is where analysis happens; the generated code is where the modernization lives; the IR is the contract between them — and it is not one substrate but two. One layer captures architectural decisions; a second captures the structural blueprint those decisions render into. The compiler principle (Pattern 7) governs the boundary between the two layers exactly as it governs the boundary between probabilistic and deterministic work generally: the first layer is where judgement is exercised and frozen, the second is a deterministic projection with no decisions left in it.

**IR-Domain holds architectural intent.** It captures the architectural commitments the modernization is making: what aggregates exist (clusters of domain objects treated as a single consistency unit), what commands they accept (instructions the system can receive), what events they emit (facts the system records about what happened), what handlers process them, what sagas coordinate work across aggregates, and what side-effect surface each piece touches. This is the tactical-design vocabulary of DDD — articulated by Eric Evans (2003) and elaborated by Vaughn Vernon (*Implementing Domain-Driven Design*, 2013); a reader unfamiliar with it can treat each term as a named slot in the IR and follow the worked example below. Every IR-Domain element is grounded back to the graph nodes that derived it, so the architectural commitment always traces to the evidence that motivated it.

IR-Domain is the substrate agents reason *about*: an agent proposing a translation grounds it in IR-Domain elements — the aggregate this paragraph touches, the command this transaction expresses, the events that follow. Because IR-Domain is queryable and reviewable, humans validate architectural commitments at this layer *before any scaffold is rendered* — which is what makes the architecture a decision rather than an emergent accident of generation.

Crucially, IR-Domain is not architecturally neutral: it *encodes the commitment*. A `HandlerIntent` commits the bounded context to [Vertical Slice](#gl-vertical-slice) Architecture, a `PortIntent` to Hexagonal, a `SagaIntent` to event-driven coordination — hard decisions that structure the most important constructs of the generated code. They are not model-mutable; they are validated by the architect during strategic recovery (Part I) and frozen into IR-Domain before any rendering begins. Which commitment is appropriate for which bounded context is the subject of Pattern 9; IR-Domain is where the chosen commitment is *recorded*.

**IR-Scaffold holds the structural blueprint.** It captures class layouts, file paths, project structure, namespace organisation, interface definitions, test-stub locations, and the `scaffold-meta.json` constitutional contracts the harness (Pattern 22) enforces. IR-Scaffold is a deterministic projection from finalized IR-Domain plus target conventions: there are no decisions in it. Given the IR-Domain and chosen emitter (Pattern 10), the scaffold is what it is.

The boundary between IR-Domain and IR-Scaffold is the boundary between deliberative work and compilation, and keeping the two layers distinct is the heart of this pattern. Collapsing them is the most common way the compiler principle silently fails: when agents can revise scaffold structure to accommodate their translations, architectural commitments stop being commitments — the system looks like it has an architecture, but the architecture is whatever the last agent run found convenient. The two-layer split is precisely what makes the structural constraints of Pattern 7 — method signatures, interface contracts, the side-effect surface a unit may touch — *enforceable*: those constraints live in IR-Scaffold as a typed shell the agent fills but cannot reshape, which is why a method that reads or writes the wrong resource fails to compile rather than merely failing review.

**A worked example.** Consider a COBOL paragraph in a claims system, `2100-VALIDATE-CLAIM`: it validates a claim against its policy and writes the result, with an audit row on failure. Analysis (Patterns 3–6) has placed it in the *Claims* bounded context and recovered its side-effect surface (reads `POLICY-MASTER`, reads `CLAIM-IN`, writes `CLAIM-VALID-OUT`, writes `AUDIT-LOG`). The IR-Domain projection records this as a `HandlerIntent`. The authoritative form is the typed model the compiler consumes, serialized to a versioned YAML artifact — and that serialization is what the architect reads, diffs, and reviews:

```yaml
# IR-DOMAIN — architectural intent, serialized for review and version control.
# Recovered from analysis, reviewed and frozen by the architect. No code yet.
handler_intent:
  name:       ValidateClaim          # canonical name from the ontology,
                                      # not the legacy's 2100-VALIDATE-CLAIM
  aggregate:  Claim                   # the consistency boundary it touches
  accepts:    ValidateClaimCommand
  emits:
    - ClaimValidated
    - ClaimRejected
  side_effects:                       # recovered from the graph, not invented
    reads:  [PolicyMaster, ClaimRecord]
    writes: [ValidationResult, AuditLog]
  grounded_in:                        # provenance back to the graph (P3)
    - graph://para/2100-VALIDATE-CLAIM
  status: frozen                      # the architect has ratified this commitment
```

The architect reviews *this* — at the level of "is `ValidateClaim` a command on the `Claim` aggregate, and are these the right events?" — and freezes it; the YAML lands in version control, so the architectural decision is diffable and auditable like any other source artifact. It describes no executable behaviour, only intent. The YAML deserializes into the typed `HandlerIntent` the compiler consumes, and from that frozen intent IR-Scaffold projects deterministically: a `ValidateClaimHandler` class in the `Claims` project, a method signature whose parameter and return types are the command and events, a typed read/write surface permitting exactly the four resources, and a test stub. What IR-Scaffold renders, concretely, is a shell like this:


```csharp
// IR-SCAFFOLD output — structure is frozen. Fill the body only.
public sealed class ValidateClaimHandler
{
    // Side-effect surface projected from the HandlerIntent above; the typed
    // dependencies permit exactly the four resources the legacy paragraph touched.
    public ValidateClaimHandler(
        IReadOnly<PolicyMaster> policies,   // read  POLICY-MASTER
        IReadOnly<ClaimRecord> claims,      // read  CLAIM-IN
        IWriter<ValidationResult> results,  // write CLAIM-VALID-OUT
        IAuditLog audit) { /* ... */ }      // write AUDIT-LOG

    public ClaimValidationOutcome Handle(ValidateClaimCommand command)
    {
        throw new NotImplementedException(); // ← agent writes the algorithm here
    }
}
```

The two code blocks are the two layers made visible: the first is *intent*, authored by recovery and ratified by a human; the second is *structure*, projected from it with no decision left to make. The agent enters only at the third step, writing the body of `Handle` — and only that. The judgement happened at IR-Domain, the structure was compiled into IR-Scaffold, and the probabilistic work is confined to the algorithm inside a shell it cannot reshape. The IR is what made those three things separable.

**The vocabulary comes from the ontology, not the legacy.** When IR-Domain captures an aggregate as `Customer`, that name comes from the canonical [ubiquitous language](#gl-ubiquitous-language) the modernization team has validated — not from whatever the legacy happened to call it (`CUST-MAST-REC`, `CMR`, `CUSTREC01`). Where the ontology is not yet established, IR-Domain carries provisional names from vocabulary inference, marked explicitly as provisional and awaiting validation. The data layer's DDL informs aggregate-boundary discovery: foreign keys reveal containment relationships, NOT NULL and CHECK constraints capture invariants that become type-level guarantees in the rendered code. IR-Domain is one of the principal consumers of ontology recovery (Pattern 4) — without it, the architecture would encode the legacy's accumulated confusion in modern syntax, which is the *Jobol* failure named in Pattern 7.

**The rendering is deterministic and programmable.** The projection from IR-Domain through IR-Scaffold to code is explicit and reproducible; in the reference implementation it runs through Roslyn, whose value here is that it exposes the compiler as a programmable API. The implication is what matters: rendering rules are *programs the team writes and tests*, not configurations a black-box tool interprets. Each architectural commitment in IR-Domain has a corresponding generator that renders it into IR-Scaffold, which drives the emitted files. The principle does not depend on Roslyn — it holds for any target language whose tooling can construct code as a syntax tree rather than as interpolated text — but Roslyn is the concrete instance the catalog's examples assume.

This is what makes changing architectural style tractable without re-running analysis. Moving a bounded context from vertical-slice to hexagonal is not a prompt change and not a re-derivation from the graph: it is writing new generators that consume the *same* IR-Domain types and emit a different IR-Scaffold shape. IR-Domain stays stable; the rendering changes. That stability is the precondition for Pattern 10 (*Pluggable Emitters*) — architecture diversity at the rendering layer, not at the analysis layer. And the split extends beyond code: the same IR-Domain feeds documentation emitters — C4 views, aggregate maps, [context maps](#gl-context-map), ubiquitous-language glossaries — each its own deterministic projection from the same substrate. Once IR-Domain's vocabulary is stable, it supports code generation, documentation rendering, and architecture-decision records from a single source of truth.

**IR-Domain is, in precise terms, an internal DSL for architectural intent — and a semantic model in Fowler's sense.** The construct vocabulary — `HandlerIntent`, `PortIntent`, `SagaIntent` and their kin, each a typed object with a fixed architectural meaning — is an *internal* (embedded) DSL in Martin Fowler's taxonomy: not a separate parsed syntax but a domain-specific vocabulary hosted in the implementation language, expressing intent above the code it renders to. A *populated* IR-Domain is what Fowler calls the **semantic model**: the model the DSL exists to populate and the thing rendering runs over. The fit is exact — a DSL is a readable way of populating a semantic model, and the semantic model drives the computation; IR-Domain is both (the YAML shown earlier is that model serialized for review, not a second source of truth). This is the structural-harness thesis (Pattern 7) made concrete: because the conceptual model lives in a designed language rather than in prose, an agent reads the IR-Domain element and its grounding instead of a prose explanation of what an aggregate is. The language *is* the context, and being typed and queryable rather than narrated, it does not drift between runs.

One property makes this an unusual DSL, and it is worth naming. In the ordinary case a human *authors* a program in the DSL — you write the SQL, you write the build script. Here the program is largely *recovered*: analysis (Patterns 3–6) projects a candidate IR-Domain out of the legacy, and the architect validates and corrects it rather than writing it from a blank page. IR-Domain is a DSL whose programs are read out of forty-year-old COBOL and ratified, not composed fresh — which is exactly why the catalog's authorship discipline (agents propose, the human decides, the substrate freezes) attaches to it: the recovered program is a proposal until a human accepts it.

A note on naming. The discipline-neutral name used throughout this catalog is *IR-Domain*. The Rosetta implementation calls its instance *WolverineIntentModel*, because [Wolverine](#gl-wolverine) handlers are what it most directly renders into — a convention for the code, not the concept. Readers applying the principle elsewhere should think *IR-Domain*; WolverineIntentModel is reported only for traceability to Rosetta.

### Consequences

Analysis and generation evolve independently. New patterns extend IR-Domain without touching the emitter; new targets add emitters without touching the analysis. IR-Domain itself becomes the durable artifact of what the modernization understands about the legacy — the thing that survives when the engagement ends and the one place an auditor can read the architecture without reading either the legacy or the generated code. Any generated artifact traces back through its IR-Domain origin to the graph nodes and ontology terms that grounded it, so architectural decisions are inspectable at the IR-Domain layer separately from the rendered code, each layer independently auditable.

The split between IR-Domain and IR-Scaffold makes the deterministic/probabilistic boundary visible at the IR layer itself, not only at the prompt-to-scaffold transition. When something goes wrong, it is clear which substrate is implicated: architectural confusion shows up in IR-Domain (wrong aggregate boundaries, missing events, malformed sagas), where it can be debugged at the level of commitments; rendering errors show up in IR-Scaffold (wrong file structure, missing test stubs, malformed scaffold metadata), where they can be debugged at the level of emission. A monolithic IR would force every diagnostic to traverse both concerns at once, which is exactly what makes undifferentiated intermediates hard to maintain.

The cost is a layer that looks dull but is not. The IR has to *actually* be typed — real schema with real validation on both sides of the split, not a loose dictionary passed between stages. The grounding back to graph nodes has to be maintained through every transformation. And IR-Domain's vocabulary has to stay aligned with the architectural intent the analysis is trying to capture, which means the IR's design is itself an ongoing concern rather than a one-time decision — the price of a contract that both sides can depend on is that someone has to tend it.

Two lineages meet in IR-Domain. From compiler theory comes the typed intermediate — LLVM IR (Lattner & Adve, 2004), GCC's GIMPLE — establishing decades ago that separating front-end analysis from back-end generation requires an intermediate in between. From the domain-specific-language tradition comes the rest: Fowler and Parsons' *Domain-Specific Languages* (Addison-Wesley, 2010) names the internal-versus-external distinction and the *semantic model* a DSL populates, which is precisely what a populated IR-Domain is. IR-Domain is a back-end compiler intermediate seen from the generation side and a front-end DSL's semantic model seen from the intent side. What this catalog adds is the framing of that intermediate as the contract between *agentic* analysis and *deterministic* generation — and the recovered-not-authored property, a DSL whose programs are read out of legacy code and validated rather than composed. The principle generalises to any agentic modernization where what is discovered must be rendered into something that compiles.

### Related patterns

Pattern 7 (*The Compiler Principle*) is what motivates the IR's existence — the IR is where the principle's deterministic/probabilistic boundary becomes a typed artifact, and the structural constraints Pattern 7 describes are enforced in IR-Scaffold. Pattern 3 (*The Graph as Projection*) is the analysis substrate the IR-Domain layer projects from. Pattern 4 (*Domain Ontology as Independent Substrate*) provides the canonical vocabulary the IR uses — without ontology, the IR risks encoding the legacy's confusion in modern syntax. Pattern 9 (*Tier-Aware Scaffolding*) decides which architectural commitment each bounded context's IR-Domain should record. Pattern 10 (*Pluggable Emitters*) is what the IR enables on the generation side: architecture diversity at the rendering layer and documentation diversity at the view layer, with IR-Domain as the stable contract for both. Pattern 23 (*The Control Plane*) surfaces IR fragments to architects during review, making architectural commitments visible before generation begins.

-----

## Pattern 9: Tier-Aware Scaffolding

### Context

The previous two patterns established a principle and an artifact: deterministic structure constrains probabilistic generation (Pattern 7), and the architectural commitment that structure encodes lives in a typed intermediate representation that records, for each [bounded context](#gl-bounded-context), *which* commitment was made — a `HandlerIntent` for vertical-slice, a `PortIntent` for hexagonal, a `SagaIntent` for event-driven coordination (Pattern 8). That left one question deliberately open: *which commitment is right for which bounded context?* IR-Domain records the decision; it does not make it. This pattern is how the decision is made.

The setting is a modernization pipeline producing scaffolds for many bounded contexts within one legacy estate. The contexts are not equal. Some are the core of the business — the pricing engine, the underwriting rules, the thing the company actually competes on — and will be read, changed, and reasoned about for the life of the system. Others are generic supporting machinery — a reference-data lookup, an address validator, an audit-logging utility — that the business needs but does not differentiate on and would happily buy off the shelf if it could. These two kinds of context deserve different architectures, and the pipeline has to know which is which before it renders anything.

### Problem

Most modernization platforms apply a single architectural template across the entire codebase, and the result is wrong almost everywhere at once. Generic domains get over-engineered — full [hexagonal architecture](#gl-hexagonal-architecture), ports and adapters and a domain core, wrapped around a lookup table, is structure no one will ever benefit from and everyone will have to maintain. Core domains get under-engineered — a pricing engine rendered as flat [vertical slices](#gl-vertical-slice) has nowhere to put the invariants that hold it together, and it collapses under its own weight within a few years as every change ripples through slices that should have been protected behind a domain boundary. The same template that is too heavy for the lookup table is too thin for the pricing engine. Uniformity guarantees that the parts of the system that matter least carry the most ceremony and the parts that matter most carry the least protection.

The pull toward uniform architecture is real, and it is operational, not naive: one scaffold mode means the renderer has a single path to maintain, every team works the same way, and onboarding is consistent. A modernization running under deadline pressure reaches for uniformity because it is *simpler to operate* — and inherits, in exchange, a codebase miscalibrated in both directions. The cost is not visible at generation time, when uniform output looks clean and consistent. It arrives years later, as maintenance burden on the trivial code and as architectural collapse in the critical code, by which point the template decision is buried under a million lines and effectively permanent.

### Forces

Architectural complexity has real costs and real benefits, and both scale with the same two variables: how much the code matters, and how often it changes. The costs are concrete — more abstractions to hold in mind, more indirection to read through, more seams where a bug can hide. So are the benefits — clearer boundaries, safer evolution, invariants that hold under change. For a stable, trivial capability the costs dominate and the benefits never arrive; for a volatile, critical one the benefits dominate and the costs are paid back many times over. A single architecture across the whole codebase forces one point on this trade-off curve onto contexts sitting at every point along it.

A second force is that the decision must be *made by humans but executed without them*. Which contexts are core is a strategic judgement — it depends on where the business competes, where it expects to change, what it would never outsource — and no static analysis recovers it reliably, because importance is a fact about the business, not about the code. Yet the scaffold selection that follows from the judgement has to run deterministically, thousands of times, with no human in the per-context loop. The pattern therefore has to separate a judgement that is made once and recorded from a selection that is executed mechanically — which is exactly the discipline Patterns 7 and 8 already established for architectural decisions generally, applied here to the choice of architecture itself.

A note on a possible confusion of vocabulary. Pattern 7 spoke of three *tiers* of work — architecture, structural constraints, business-logic algorithm — distinguishing what is compiled from what is generated *within* a single unit. This pattern's *tiers* are a different axis entirely: they classify *whole bounded contexts* by domain weight, to decide which architectural shape each is rendered into. The two are orthogonal. Every context, whatever its domain tier, has its architecture compiled and its business logic generated; the domain tier decides only *which* architecture gets compiled. Where this pattern says "tier," it means domain weight, in the sense Evans gives it.

### Pattern

Annotate each bounded context with a domain tier reflecting its importance and likely rate of change, and let the tier drive scaffold selection deterministically. The classification is the human judgement; the selection is the mechanical consequence.

The taxonomy is calibrated to CICS COBOL modernization, but the underlying distinction is Eric Evans' classification of subdomains in *Domain-Driven Design* (2003): some subdomains are **core** — what differentiates the business; some are **supporting** — necessary to the core but not themselves differentiating; some are **generic** — capabilities the business needs but could obtain anywhere. Evans' point is strategic: invest design effort in the core, spend as little as defensible on the generic, and treat the supporting subdomains as the pragmatic middle. What Evans does *not* say is which tactical architecture fits which subdomain type — he is silent on the mapping from subdomain weight to code shape. That mapping is this catalog's contribution, and it is offered as a calibrated heuristic rather than a derivation: the form of architectural investment should track the domain tier, and the three scaffold shapes below are the starting points the Rosetta prototype has exercised. Engagement experience will refine the boundaries between them.

**Tier 0–1 — generic and supporting-leaning subdomains: vertical-slice scaffold, or no scaffold at all.** Reference-data lookups, validation rules, audit logging, format conversions — capabilities that are stable, well-understood, and not where the business competes. The scaffold is a vertical slice: input to processing to output, no shared domain abstractions, nothing to navigate. The code is easy to read and cheap to change precisely because it carries no structure it does not need. At the extreme, where the capability is stable enough that a rewrite is not justified at all, the right answer is *no scaffold*: Pattern 28 (*Replatform with Modern Facade*) keeps the legacy code running behind a generated facade, and the scaffolding concept never enters. The cheapest architecture for a [generic subdomain](#gl-generic-subdomain) is sometimes none.

**Tier 2 — core subdomains of moderate complexity: hybrid scaffold.** Business logic that genuinely matters and will change, but is not so deeply interconnected that it needs full isolation. The scaffold is hybrid: vertical slices handle request flow at the edge, while a lightweight domain model holds the business logic that several slices share. This is the middle ground — more structure than a generic capability deserves, less ceremony than the crown jewels require. In the engagements examined so far, Tier 2 dominates: most code is moderately important business logic that does not warrant full hexagonal isolation but is too valuable to flatten into independent slices. The hybrid scaffold has earned its place by being the most-used, which is itself a finding — a modernization that offered only "thin" and "heavy" options would mis-serve the bulk of a real estate.

**Tier 3 — the strategic core, the crown jewels: full hexagonal scaffold.** The pricing engine, the underwriting rules, the handful of contexts that *are* the business. The scaffold is hexagonal: the domain model at the centre, surrounded by ports and adapters that keep infrastructure concerns from leaking into it. This is the structure that pays its tax over the system's whole lifetime — it costs more to build and read, and it earns that cost back every time the core changes without a database detail or a transport concern forcing its way into the domain logic. This is where Evans' insistence on protecting the [core domain](#gl-core-domain) from infrastructural contamination earns its keep, and where the IR-Domain `PortIntent` of Pattern 8 comes from.

**Where the tier comes from, and who decides it.** The classification lives in the graph as an annotation on each bounded context, and it is produced the same way every other architectural decision in this catalog is produced: agents propose, the architect decides, the substrate freezes. Heuristics give a first cut — cyclomatic complexity and coupling from static analysis, change frequency from version-control history where it is available, the capability map's strategic classification from Pattern 1, the side-effect surface from Pattern 5. These signals *suggest* a tier; they cannot settle it, because the deciding variable — does the business compete here, will this change — is strategic knowledge no metric captures. So the heuristic tier is a proposal an architect reviews and ratifies, after which it is frozen into the graph and read deterministically by the emitter. It is not a model decision, and it is not a metric's decision; it is a human judgement that metrics inform. This is the capability map of Pattern 1 made operational at the scaffold layer: Pattern 1 classifies the capability strategically, this pattern turns the classification into a rendered architecture.

The unit being tiered is the *recovered* bounded context, not the legacy program — and on a mainframe those are rarely the same thing. A COBOL estate does not divide its code by domain weight; a single 8,000-line program can hold crown-jewel pricing logic and a generic date-conversion routine in adjacent paragraphs, sharing the same copybooks and working storage. Tiering presupposes that the boundary recovery of Patterns 5 and 6 has already happened: the context whose tier is being annotated is a *logical* unit the analysis has carved out, which may cut across several programs or sit inside one. Where a program spans tiers, the slice must come before the tier — the crown-jewel logic is separated from the utility code (Pattern 5) so each can be tiered and scaffolded on its own terms. Annotating a tier on a program that still fuses core and generic logic only pushes the miscalibration the pattern exists to prevent down one level: the scaffold is then too heavy for half the program and too thin for the other half. The tier attaches cleanly only once the boundary does.

Once the tier is frozen, the rest is mechanical. The emitter (Pattern 10) selects the scaffold variant from the tier annotation with no further judgement, and the selected shape is recorded as the architectural commitment in IR-Domain (Pattern 8) — closing the loop the cluster opened: the principle says architecture is compiled not generated (Pattern 7), the IR records which architecture (Pattern 8), and this pattern is the policy that chose it.

### Consequences

The architecture earns its complexity. Where structure is justified, the system pays for it; where it is not, the system does not. Generic supporting code stays simple and cheap to maintain; core code receives the structural investment its longevity warrants. The codebase, read across all its contexts, carries complexity proportional to importance rather than uniform complexity proportional to nothing — which is the single biggest lever on long-term maintenance cost that the modernization controls.

The cost is the classification itself, and it is a real cost in two directions. Mis-tiering downward — calling a core context generic — renders the crown jewels as flat slices and reproduces the under-engineering the pattern exists to prevent; the error surfaces years later as architectural collapse. Mis-tiering upward — calling a generic context core — burdens a lookup table with hexagonal ceremony; the error surfaces immediately as wasted effort and ongoing maintenance drag, which is the cheaper mistake but still a mistake. Because the heuristics only propose and cannot decide, the quality of the tiering rests on the architect's strategic judgement, and a modernization that treats tier annotation as a box-ticking step rather than a strategic one will mis-tier systematically.

The three-tier policy is also more specific than the principle beneath it, and should be held more loosely. The durable claim is *match architectural investment to domain weight*; the specific mapping of three subdomain types to three scaffold shapes is a calibrated heuristic, not a law. Some estates will need finer gradations, different scaffold shapes, or different boundaries between tiers, and the catalog expects the mapping to be re-calibrated per engagement. What does not change is the discipline of making the trade-off consciously, per context, rather than imposing one architecture everywhere by default.

A final consequence worth naming: tiers are not permanent. A context that is supporting today can become core when the business changes its competitive footing, and a core context can decay to supporting once a capability commoditises. Because the tier is an explicit annotation in the graph rather than an implicit property of the code, re-tiering is a tractable operation — change the annotation, re-render the scaffold, migrate the business logic into the new shape — rather than an archaeological dig through a codebase whose architecture was frozen at first generation. The tier annotation makes architectural evolution a decision the modernization can revisit, which is itself part of matching architecture to a business that does not stand still.

### Related patterns

Pattern 1 (*Business-Aligned Capability Strategy*) is the strategic source of the tier: it classifies each capability core / supporting / generic, and this pattern turns that classification into a rendered architecture. Pattern 7 (*The Compiler Principle*) is what makes tier-based scaffolding tractable — the deterministic emitter selects scaffolds from tier annotations with no model involvement. Pattern 8 (*The Intermediate Representation*) is where the chosen architectural commitment is recorded once selected; this pattern decides what Pattern 8 records. Pattern 10 (*Pluggable Emitters*) is what makes the scaffold variants implementable as separate, swappable emitters. Pattern 5 (*Slice and Seam Discovery*) attaches a provisional tier estimate to each slice candidate, which this pattern's classification confirms or revises. Pattern 28 (*Replatform with Modern Facade*) is the Tier-0 floor — the case where the right scaffold is none. The *Jobol* antipattern names what happens when tiering is skipped and every paragraph is rendered into the same shape regardless of strategic weight; the *Frozen Architecture* antipattern names what happens when scaffold selection inherits the legacy's accidental decomposition instead of designing the right shape per tier.

-----

## Pattern 10: Pluggable Emitters

### Context

Pattern 9 closed on a claim it deferred: tier policy is *implemented as emitter selection* — tier-0 routes to a vertical-slice emitter, tier-3 to a hexagonal one, tier-2 to a hybrid. That sentence assumed a pipeline in which the target architecture is one of several possible architectures, chosen late and swapped freely. This pattern is what makes that assumption hold.

The choice of target depends on the engagement. Some teams want [vertical slice](#gl-vertical-slice) architecture (VSA) with [Wolverine](#gl-wolverine) handlers; others want [hexagonal architecture](#gl-hexagonal-architecture) with explicit ports and adapters; others want a Java target instead of C#; others want a hybrid for tier-2 [bounded contexts](#gl-bounded-context) that balances VSA velocity with hexagonal protection. The target may also evolve as understanding of the legacy improves or as priorities shift across the life of the modernization. The pipeline cannot be built around any one of these shapes without betraying the others.

The same pipeline must also serve constituencies that consume something other than code. Architects reviewing [strategic design](#gl-strategic-design) need C4 model views; data engineers need entity-relationship diagrams; DDD practitioners checking [aggregate](#gl-aggregate) boundaries need aggregate maps and [context maps](#gl-context-map); operators tracing state need event maps; regulators auditing decisions need architecture decision records. Each constituency wants a different view of the same architecture.

### Problem

If the target architecture is hard-coded into the pipeline, switching targets requires rebuilding the pipeline. Teams either commit to one architecture upfront and live with the consequences, or they fork the pipeline per target and maintain divergent toolchains. Both options are operationally expensive.

If the target architecture is configured but the configuration is shallow — a few flags, a few template variants — the pipeline supports limited variation around a single target shape but can’t address fundamentally different targets. A pipeline configured to produce vertical slice variants can’t suddenly produce hexagonal scaffolds; the configuration vocabulary is too narrow to express the structural difference.

The deeper issue is that target architecture is a first-class decision that should live somewhere identifiable in the pipeline’s design, not as a side effect of how the pipeline was constructed.

The same problem applies to documentation. Hand-authored documentation drifts. The diagrams produced for kickoff are inaccurate by month three; the wiki page that explained the architecture is stale by month six; the C4 model the architect drew on the whiteboard exists only in someone’s screenshot folder. When the system changes — and it always changes — the documentation lags or fragments until the gap between system and documentation becomes unbridgeable, and the team gives up.

Auto-generated documentation from code has a different problem: it produces documentation at the wrong level of abstraction. Class diagrams that mirror code structure are useless for strategic understanding. Sequence diagrams generated from method calls capture mechanics but not intent. The strategic constituencies need views that exist *above* the code, not below it.

### Forces

Different engagements have different correct targets for code. The same legacy may be modernized for different audiences with different architectural preferences — a bank’s risk-engine team may want hexagonal for strategic-core contexts; the same bank’s commodity reporting team may want lift-and-shift to VSA. Forcing a single target across all uses limits the pipeline’s reach.

Multiple constituencies each want a different view of the architecture. Each view changes when the architecture changes. Manual maintenance is unsustainable across constituencies and over time. Auto-generation from code produces views at the wrong abstraction level for most constituencies.

But supporting multiple targets — code or documentation — requires architectural discipline: the pipeline must factor cleanly enough that the target-specific logic is replaceable. Every concession to target-agnosticism in the analysis side is a constraint on what the IR can express. Every concession to target-specificity in the emitter side is duplication when targets share structure.

The substrates that produced the code (graph, IR, ontology) already encode the strategic and tactical decisions — but they encode them as data, not as visual or narrative documentation. The same substrates that feed code generation can feed documentation generation if the architecture allows it.

### Pattern

Make the emitter pluggable. The graph and the IR (Pattern 8) are target-agnostic; they describe the legacy and its architectural intent without committing to a specific output shape. The emitter is target-specific; it consumes the IR and produces an output in whatever target form is appropriate.

A new target requires a new emitter, not a new pipeline. The same graph, fed to the same IR, fed to a different emitter, produces a different output. The architectural decision lives in the choice of emitter — a deliberate, visible, replaceable choice — not in the pipeline’s foundations.

Emitters fall into two categories that share the same compiler discipline.

**Code emitters** generate the modernized system itself. An emitter is responsible for the whole shape of the scaffold: directory structure, project files, dependency wiring, test scaffolds, build configuration, deployment metadata. A VSA emitter generates one shape; a hexagonal emitter generates another; a hybrid emitter generates a third. Each is its own Roslyn code generator — or, for non-C# targets, its own equivalent in the target language’s tooling.

In Rosetta today, code emitters exist for vertical slice architecture (Wolverine, C#) and for hexagonal architecture (Wolverine + explicit ports, C#), with a hybrid emitter for tier-2 contexts that combines VSA velocity with hexagonal protection at specific [seams](#gl-seam). A *facade emitter* extends the same architecture to the replatform strategy (Pattern 28): rather than rendering scaffolds the agents fill with translated logic, the facade emitter renders C# wrappers around Raincode-compiled COBOL artifacts, exposing the legacy capability through modern protocols and canonical vocabulary without generating any business logic. Future emitters could target Java with Spring Boot for organizations standardised on JVM stacks; alternative .NET frameworks for event-sourced workloads where Wolverine isn’t the best fit; or even non-handler architectures like CQRS-with-separate-read-models for analytical bounded contexts.

**Documentation emitters render architectural views from the same substrates — and they are where this pattern crosses the compiler-principle line.** The code emitters above are deterministic: a Roslyn generator renders the same scaffold from the same IR every time. Documentation divides in two, on opposite sides of Pattern 7's boundary. *Structural views* — C4 component diagrams, entity-relationship diagrams, aggregate maps, [context maps](#gl-context-map) in Evans' 2003 sense — are deterministic projections in the same way code is: a graph-and-IR traversal rendered to PlantUML or Mermaid, where node X with edge Y becomes box X with arrow Y, idempotently. *Narrative views* — the prose that explains *why* a bounded context exists, the rationale in an architecture-decision record, the definitions (not just the term list) in a [ubiquitous-language](#gl-ubiquitous-language) glossary — are not mechanical projections at all. Writing accurate, readable prose from the substrates is the language-transformation work Pattern 7 assigns to the model: an agent navigating and interpreting the GraphRAG (Pattern 3), not a template. This emitter is therefore agentic and non-deterministic, and it carries the same discipline as agentic code translation — every claim grounded in the substrate node that supports it (the *analysis, not interpretation* rule of Pattern 3, which is what keeps generated documentation from confabulating architecture that isn't there), and the output reviewed rather than trusted. Regenerating a structural view is idempotent; regenerating a narrative view produces a fresh draft to be re-reviewed, not a bit-identical artifact.

The documentation emitter is *planned, not yet built* in Rosetta. The design intent is the agent-driven GraphRAG-navigation approach described above, producing — among others — C4 model views, ER diagrams, aggregate and context maps, ubiquitous-language glossaries grounded in the ontology of Pattern 4, and architecture-decision records linked from the spec deltas of Pattern 23. The intended output lives in markdown and standard diagram formats (PlantUML, Mermaid, SVG) so it integrates with the engineering workflow: versioned in Git beside the code, viewable in pull requests, rebuilt in pipelines. The principle the built code emitters establish — that what gets emitted is a replaceable consumer of stable substrates — is what the documentation emitter is designed to extend; whether the agentic narrative emitter holds its grounding discipline at scale is a hypothesis the catalog has not yet tested.

The IR’s job is to remain stable across emitter additions, code or documentation. When a new emitter is added, the IR shouldn’t need new types — the existing IR types (HandlerIntent, AggregateIntent, CommandIntent, EventIntent, BoundedContextIntent) should be expressive enough that the new emitter can interpret them in its own architectural or documentation idiom. If a new emitter requires new IR types, that’s a signal the IR was implicitly biased toward existing targets.

When humans need to *change* the architecture, they don’t edit the documentation directly. They edit the substrates — refining the ontology, updating the IR, adjusting the graph annotations — and the documentation re-renders to reflect the change. This is what Cyrille Martraire (*Living Documentation*, 2019) called the discipline of making documentation a byproduct of doing the work. This pattern operationalises that discipline through the same compiler architecture that produces the code.

### Consequences

The architectural decision lives in the choice of emitter, not in the graph or the IR or the agents. That decision becomes explicit, reviewable, replaceable. Teams modernize their codebase against the architecture they want, not against the one the pipeline imposed by accident.

Documentation, once the emitter is built, need not go stale relative to the system it describes. Reviewers validate decisions at the abstraction level appropriate to them: architects check the C4 model, data engineers check the ER diagrams, DDD practitioners check aggregate and context maps. Each constituency engages with documentation generated from the same source of truth that produced the code — deterministically for the structural views, and as a grounded, reviewed agent draft for the narrative ones. The substrates themselves are the canonical record; diagrams and prose are convenience views derived from them. This inverts the traditional relationship: substrates are not artifacts derived from documentation; documentation is artifacts derived from substrates.

Emitters compose into a library that grows over time. The first engagement contributes the first emitter; the second engagement may reuse it or add a variant. After several engagements, the catalog of emitters covers the most common code targets and documentation views, and new engagements typically need to extend an existing emitter rather than write a new one from scratch. The investment compounds across the engagement portfolio.

The cost is the discipline of keeping the IR target-agnostic. Every IR concept must be expressible in any reasonable target architecture and renderable in any reasonable documentation view, which constrains the IR’s vocabulary. Adding a new target may reveal that the IR has been quietly target-specific in places, requiring refactoring. The IR’s stability across emitter additions is something to maintain deliberately, not assume.

There is a second cost the library framing hides: each emitter is a substantial engineering investment in its own right. A code emitter that produces production-quality C# — handling edge cases the IR carries, respecting target-framework conventions, surviving the variety of bounded contexts a real engagement contains — is not a weekend project. The VSA, hexagonal, and hybrid emitters in Rosetta took months each. The documentation emitters being designed will take comparable effort. The compounding benefit across engagements is real, but the upfront investment per emitter is real too.

A third cost is *emitter divergence*. When two emitters drift in what they assume about the IR — emitter A treats a particular `HandlerIntent` field as optional, emitter B treats it as required — the result is silent miscompilation: the IR validates, both emitters run, but the outputs encode different architectural commitments. The catalog must include test infrastructure that exercises every emitter against the same IR corpus and flags divergence at the integration boundary. Without that, the pluggability claim is theoretical; with it, the pluggability claim has operational cost.

Reviewers in the control plane (Pattern 23) can request a specific view to validate a decision. Spec deltas (Pattern 23) include the documentation re-renders as part of the change package; reviewers see what the architecture looked like before, what it looks like after, and which substrate changes produced the difference.

Pluggable target architectures are a long-established practice in compiler back-ends — LLVM (Lattner & Adve, 2004) is the canonical modern example, with a single front-end feeding multiple architecture-specific back-ends for x86, ARM, RISC-V, and so on. What this catalog contributes is two things. First, the application of the back-end pluggability principle to *architectural styles* rather than to *machine architectures*: the back-end isn’t a CPU instruction set, it’s an architectural pattern (VSA, hexagonal, event-sourced). Second, the extension of the same substrate-stable / emitter-replaceable discipline to *documentation* as another emitter target — with a twist the compiler analogy does not anticipate: a back-end is purely deterministic, but the documentation emitter is split, its structural views deterministic and its narrative views agentic, an LLM interpreting the GraphRAG under the same grounding discipline as code translation. The pluggability principle is established by the built code emitters; the documentation emitter is its designed but not-yet-built extension, and the agentic narrative half is the part still to be proven.

### Related patterns

Pattern 7 (*The Compiler Principle*) is what makes pluggable emitters possible: deterministic emission is what’s being plugged in. Pattern 8 (*The Intermediate Representation*) is what the emitters consume — and is what must remain target-agnostic for pluggability to work. Pattern 9 (*Tier-Aware Scaffolding*) is one application of the pluggable-emitter pattern: tier policy is implemented as emitter selection (tier-0/1 uses VSA emitter; tier-3 uses hexagonal emitter; tier-2 uses hybrid). Pattern 3 (*The Graph as Projection*) and Pattern 4 (*Domain Ontology as Independent Substrate*) are the substrates documentation emitters consume alongside the IR. Pattern 23 (*The Control Plane*) is where rendered documentation surfaces for human consumption and where spec deltas integrate documentation re-renders into change packages. The *Frozen Architecture* antipattern names what happens when the emitter is hard-coded into the pipeline and architectural decisions stop being explicit, reviewable, replaceable.

-----

## Pattern 11: Commands and Events as the Logical Boundary

### Context

The patterns to this point have produced a modernized system as a set of [bounded contexts](#gl-bounded-context), each rendered into a scaffold whose architecture was chosen by tier (Pattern 9) and emitted by a target-specific emitter (Pattern 10). One decision the emitter made, almost in passing, now needs to be examined on its own: *how do these contexts talk to each other?* Pattern 10 noted that an emitter could target in-process [Wolverine](#gl-wolverine) or distributed Wolverine — two physical shapes of the same communication. This pattern is about why that choice must stay a deployment-time decision and never harden into the architecture — and about how, once it is held open, the system's physical shape can be made to *evolve* under the same agents-propose / human-approves / substrate-enforces discipline that governs its generation.

The contexts do not stand alone. Business workflows cross them — a claim is validated in one context, priced in another, posted to the ledger in a third — and the crossing has to be expressed somehow. The expression may need to be synchronous and in-process at first, when the system ships as a single deployable and transactional simplicity is worth more than independent scaling. It may need to become asynchronous and distributed later, when scaling pressure, team-boundary alignment, or operational independence make the monolith the constraint rather than the convenience. The system's communication structure has to survive that transition without being rewritten for it — and the transition itself, repeated context by context over the system's life, is too frequent and too evidence-driven to be run as a series of hand-planned architecture projects.

### Problem

Most modernizations pick a deployment model — monolith or microservices — and bake it into the architectural structure. Cross-context communication is modeled as direct method calls in a monolith, or as REST and RPC boundaries in a distributed system, and the choice sets like concrete. Switching from method calls to messaging is a major refactor; switching back is also a major refactor. The deployment decision, made early and on the least information the project will ever have, becomes the most expensive thing to revisit.

The deeper problem is that two genuinely distinct decisions have been conflated. *What contexts communicate, and what they communicate,* is a logical question: which unit produces what command, which handles which event, what business intent the message carries. *How they communicate, and where they run,* is a physical question: synchronous or asynchronous, in-process or over a network, one deployable or many. These two questions have different right answers, and — critically — their answers change on different schedules. The logical contract between Claims and Billing is stable; it follows the business. The physical arrangement is volatile; it follows load, org structure, and operational appetite. Binding them together couples a stable thing to a volatile one, so every change in the volatile thing threatens the stable one. That is the coupling this pattern exists to break.

There is a specific casualty when the two are conflated in a mainframe modernization: the legacy's transactional guarantees. A CICS system has been preserving consistency semantics for decades — a unit of work commits or it does not. If decomposition is done by scattering method calls into network calls without a discipline that carries those semantics across, consistency drops quietly during the transition, and the failure surfaces in production as data that does not reconcile.

And there is a problem of *agency*. Even granting that the physical shape should evolve, who decides when, and on what evidence? Left to humans alone the re-architecture stalls — judging readiness means reading telemetry, coupling, change-frequency, and transaction boundaries across a large system, continuously, which is more than a team manages between feature deadlines. Left to an autonomous tool it is reckless — a topology change has consequences no one should ship unreviewed. The decision needs both machine-scale evidence-reading and human accountability, and the architecture has to be shaped so that combination is *safe* to apply.

### Forces

In-process synchronous communication is operationally simpler: fewer moving parts, easier debugging, stronger transactional guarantees, one thing to deploy. Distributed asynchronous communication is more scalable: independent deployment, fault isolation, team autonomy, the ability to scale the hot context without scaling the rest. These are not ranked goods — each is correct under different conditions. The difficulty is that most systems begin where the simpler choice is right and evolve toward where the scalable choice becomes necessary, and the evolution is gradual, per-context, and impossible to schedule in advance. The architecture must let the transition happen one context at a time, when each context's operational pressure justifies it, without an architectural rewrite at each step.

A second force pulls the other way. A logical-boundary discipline has a cost: every cross-context interaction has to be modeled as a typed command or event rather than the method call a developer would otherwise reach for. That is ceremony, and ceremony is friction — friction a team under deadline routes around unless the architecture makes routing around it harder than complying. The discipline only holds if it is enforced mechanically; left to good intentions, method-level coupling leaks back at the first inconvenient moment and the logical boundary quietly stops existing.

Third, the transactional semantics named in the Problem are themselves a force, because they constrain *how* the transition can be made. The move from in-process to distributed cannot be a pure transport swap where consistency is concerned: an in-process transaction spanning two contexts becomes, once they are separate services, a distributed-consistency problem to be solved deliberately — typically by accepting eventual consistency where the business tolerates it and preserving immediate consistency, through infrastructure, where it does not. Whether a given boundary tolerates eventual consistency is a judgment about business meaning, not a fact the code reveals.

Fourth, evolution decisions are evidence-heavy but accountability-critical. The evidence that a context is ready to extract (or that an extracted service should fold back) is the kind of large, noisy, multi-signal reading machine judgment handles well and humans handle slowly; the act of extracting is a production topology change no one should ship unreviewed. The forces point to a division of labour: probabilistic judgment reads and proposes, deterministic machinery transforms, humans hold the gate on the consequence.

### Pattern

Model communication between bounded contexts as commands and events at the logical level, independent of how they are physically implemented, and ship the system as a **modular monolith first** — then let its physical shape evolve under an agentic loop the logical boundary makes safe.

A **command** expresses *something the system should do*; an **event** expresses *something the system has observed*. Both are first-class architectural artifacts — typed, named, owned by a specific context, traceable through the audit trail — and they, not method signatures, are the public surface of a context. A context exposes what it can be asked to do and what it announces has happened, and nothing else. The discipline that makes this real is enforced by construction (Pattern 7): **no cross-context interaction may be a method call.** Every crossing goes through a command or an event; cross-context method invocation does not compile or does not resolve, so the boundary holds without reviewer vigilance. A boundary that depends on everyone remembering it is not a boundary; a boundary the compiler enforces is.

The physical implementation is then a separate, deferred, reversible decision. Initially, commands and events flow as in-process dispatches within a single modular-monolith deployable: contexts share a process, communication is synchronous, transactional boundaries are simple, and there is one thing to deploy and operate. Wolverine (or an equivalent messaging-and-handler framework) does the dispatching, but what each context publishes to its neighbours is the command-and-event vocabulary, never a method to call. *Modular monolith first* takes the operational simplicity of one deployable while keeping the logical seams that make later extraction mechanical — the deliberate refusal to pay distributed-systems cost before the system has earned the need.

**The evolution runs as an agentic loop with three kinds of work kept distinct** — the same division this catalog applies to generation (Pattern 7), now applied to topology:

- *Probabilistic judgment proposes.* An agent observes the running monolith — call volumes and latencies across seams, coupling and change-frequency per context, transaction spans, team-ownership signals — and nominates candidates: "extract *Pricing* to its own service," or "fold *Notifications* back into the monolith; its independence isn't paying." It attaches the rationale and, crucially, a reading of *consistency tolerance* at each affected seam: which crossings can move to eventual consistency and which must keep immediate consistency. This reading is judgment about business meaning, exactly the non-deterministic work an agent is suited to draft and exactly the work that must not be trusted blindly.
- *Humans on the loop review and approve.* A topology change is a production decision with operational and consistency consequences, so it is a gated promotion, never an autonomous act. The reviewer sees the nominated candidate, the evidence behind it, the consistency analysis, and — where Pattern 27's dual-run is in force — comparison evidence from running both shapes in parallel. The human approves, defers, or rejects. Agents propose; the human decides; the decision is recorded.
- *Deterministic machinery executes.* Once approved, the transformation itself is mechanical and carries no judgment. The same commands and events that flowed in-process now flow across messaging infrastructure; the receiving context's handlers are unchanged, the sending context's call sites are unchanged, and only the transport changes — which was always the emitter's concern (Pattern 10), re-rendered deterministically, not hand-edited. The substrate executes the frozen decision; it does not revisit it.

The logical boundary is what makes this loop *safe* — the heart of the pattern.
